===PAGE 63=== (ocr, 50 lines)
   1|                                                                   FINANCE COMMITTEE REPORT
   2|                                                                                      Fiscal Year 2023
   3|             Annual Town Meeting is an important event for community members to come together and vote on warrant articles that
   4|             directly impact how we manage our town. Amongst these articles is the FY23 budget, capital plan, authorizations for
   5|             various enterprise and revolving funds, and appropriations relating to stabilization funds and reserves. Throughout the
   6|             year the Town Manager and department heads carefully review expenses and have thoughtful conversations about how
   7|             to best serve and manage the town. While developing the budget they considered funding, contracts, and mandates, as
   8|             well as what the community wants and needs to thrive. The Town Manager formulated a recommended budget, which
   9|             she presented to the Finance Committee on February 17th. Over six subsequent Finance Committee meetings,
  10|             department heads presented overviews and plans relating to their budgets and capital requests. A Public Hearing was
  11|             held on March 31st, as required by the Town Charter, and the Finance Committee voted on recommendations pertaining
  12|             to financially relevant articles.
  13|             Armed with all the information gathered during this process, the Finance Committee recommends approval of the Town
  14|             Manager's omnibus budget, as outlined in this warrant.
  15|             Overall, our financial condition is good. Revenues are stable, aided by strong collections, increases in state aid, new
  16|             growth, and a variety of federal and state grants that the town has secured. On the expense side, while not meeting every
  17|             need outlined by the department heads, this budget does begin to address several emerging and previously unmet needs.
  18|              The total in requested expenditures is $49,953,531.76. While we are pleased with our current fiscal standing, we do face
  19|             challenges ahead; especially with regard to a number of our municipal buildings, which we outline in more detail below.
  20|             Notably, our certified free cash this year is $2,666,962, which is substantially higher than in previous years, a temporary
  21|             benefit resulting from appropriations that were not fully expended in FY21 as a result of COVID-related restrictions and
  22|             impacts. Town fiscal policy directs portions of free cash to accounts such as stabilization, the OPEB allocation (a fund to
  23|             pay for future retiree benefits), and the capital plan.
  24|             Accordingly, this year's recommended capital plan (funded by free cash, tax revenues, and unspent capital
  25|             appropriations from previous years) is $2,162,849. It is an unprecedented investment of available funds (without
  26|             incurring any additional debt) into the capital infrastructure that supports our town. As of the printing of this warrant, the
  27|             Finance Committee is seeking to answer a few specific questions pertaining to the capital plan. We will have a
  28|             recommendation on the capital plan, Article 6, at Town Meeting on May 7.
  29|             Other notable items:
  30|             • For FY23, the proposed appropriation for education is $23,397,371, 56.2%% of the Town's total budget, and a
  31|                  3.78% increase over last year. This includes our Monty Tech assessment at $1,054,376, which is a 20.4% increase
  32|                  for FY23, mainly due to an increase in enrollment from 83 to 94 students. Additionally, the town budget includes
  33|                  $1,207,421 in School Retiree Health Insurance. Fortunately, this year the town benefited from an $898,610 increase
  34|                  in Chapter 70 aid, which more than offsets the $673,617 increase in the Lunenburg School District budget.
  35|             While our Chapter 70 increase was welcome news, the increase was partly a result of formula changes that recognized
  36|             more low-income students at a higher tier of funding. Our vulnerable populations are of top concern for all of our
  37|             department leaders.
  38|             An area of concern for the Finance Committee are several personnel positions that the School District is funding through
  39|             ESSER 2 and ESSER 3 federal grants. These grants, totaling $1,939,868 will expire in FY24. Many of these positions
  40|             are addressing acute health, academic, and social/emotional needs that have arisen from COVID-19. Over the next two
  41|             years the School Committee will need to determine what positions we need to maintain, and how to fund them.,
  42|             • This year's budget supports an increase of $53,443 for the library budget to support the addition of two part-time
  43|                   personnel and additional expenses. This increase will enable the library to extend its operating hours to include
  44|                  opening on Fridays.
  45|             • This year's budget continues to support long-term staffing and investment programs that we committed to in
  46|             previous years, including an additional full-time firefighter and police officer. We are again increasing road pavement
  47|             management funding by $50,000. We are also contributing $450,000 to the special-purpose stabilization fund, which
  48|             should give us the option to pay cash for ambulance and fire truck replacements in coming years, as opposed to
  49|             borrowing or using free cash.
  50|                                                                                               63

===PAGE 64=== (ocr, 49 lines)
   1|             • This year's budget includes a 4.71% increase in IT spending, for a total of $316,022.89. In addition, the Lunenburg
   2|                   School District has budgeted $330,514 for technology-related administration and educational expenses. In late
   3|                  FY21, the town began the process of transferring the function of IT strategy and leadership to a private firm, which
   4|                   works in close consultation with town and school department leadership. Following a comprehensive review, an
   5|                  upgrade of networking and cloud services has commenced, which will continue into FY23. Additionally, the town is
   6|                   instituting rotational upgrades of computers and peripherals throughout our operations. The town website will also
   7|                   get regular upgrades. These programs are drastically improving the quality and reliability of our IT infrastructure and
   8|                  improving our security posture. The Finance Committee was pleased with the 'out-of-the-box' thinking and
   9|                  interdepartmental cooperation. We have encouraged all departments to find similar synergies and opportunities for
  10|                   collaboration and cost sharing.
  11|             • As we have highlighted in previous years, Lunenburg's debt ratio has been identified by an independent credit-rating
  12|                   agency as the sole reason that we are unable to achieve the highest-available bond rating. The good news is that our
  13|                  total debt and our debt service are declining. Our Excludable Debt Service to Levy Ratio is 9.18%, nearly 2% below
  14|                  the 11% cap. Our Net Regular Debt Service to Levy Ratio is 1.22% in FY 23, well-below the 4% cap in our fiscal
  15|                   policy. Our debt service will continue to decline over the next two years as bonds for the Public Safety Building,
  16|                  Primary School, and Library come off the debt schedule. However, this bit of good news could be temporary. There
  17|                  are several projects currently under discussion, which if acted upon and funded by borrowing, could exceed our debt
  18|                  limits in coming years. The Municipal Building Design Committee will likely have recommendations in coming
  19|                  months pertaining to Town Hall, Ritter, and the TC Passios building. The Select Board is considering re-
  20|                  development of the old Primary School property at 30 School Street. The Parks Commission is formulating a master
  21|                  plan for Marshall Park and is investigating issues with the Town Beach building. And the Lunenburg School District
  22|                   anticipates a renovation or replacement of Turkey Hill Elementary School within the next ten years.
  23|             • The warrant includes two articles that, if approved as presented, will make progress in addressing the old Primary
  24|                   School property at 30 School Street. Article 6, the capital plan, appropriates $371,000 for hazardous materials
  25|                   abatement. Article 19 appropriates $450,000 for the demolition of the building and engineering plans for the
  26|                  property's redevelopment into a municipal park.
  27|                During FY22, the town received $3,507,971 from the federal government resulting from The American Rescue Plan
  28|                   Act (ARPA). These funds are not accounted for in the FY23 budget. Recent guidance from the U.S. Treasury gives
  29|                  municipal executives (in our case the Select Board) fairly wide latitude for appropriating these funds. The Select
  30|                   Board will soon be gathering opinions and ideas pertaining to how we should use these funds. Potentially, some of
  31|                   this money could be used to offset costs relating to some of the projects mentioned above.
  32|             Lunenburg's overall good fiscal condition is the result of adherence to conservative fiscal policy recommended by the
  33|             Town Manager, enacted by the Select Board, and enabled by Town Meeting appropriations. There is no doubt that we
  34|             will face new challenges in the coming years. In addition to some of the concerns outlined above, inflation is a looming
  35|             headwind that will likely drive-up labor, capital, and materials costs. We believe that the investments we are making
  36|             today will give us greater flexibility and capacity to address future challenges.
  37|             The Finance Committee thanks Town Manager Heather Lemieux and Finance Director, Karen Brochu for their tireless
  38|             dedication and leadership throughout the year, and especially during budget season. We also thank our department heads
  39|             and all of our municipal and school employees for their exemplary service to the citizens of Lunenburg and stewardship
  40|             of our town.
  41|             Lunenburg Finance Committee
  42|             Peter Beardmore, Chair
  43|             Christopher Menard, Vice-Chair
  44|             Thomas Gray, Co-Clerk
  45|             Thomas Balish, Co-Clerk
  46|             Jay Simeone

===PAGE 65=== (ocr, 30 lines)
   1|                                                                       HISTORICAL COMMISSION
   2|             → The Lunenburg Historical Commission had a productive year.in FY2022. We researched and planned for a town
   3|                   wide survey of historic houses and buildings. This is requested from every community by the Massachusetts
   4|                   Historical Commission.
   5|              → Informative articles were written and published in the Lunenburg Ledger.
   6|             → The Commission advocated for the survival of 68 Elm St, which is undergoing demolition by neglect. It is a 1755
   7|                   Saltbox and barn. We also advocated for the preservation of other properties.
   8|             → The Commission facilitated the Demolition Delay bylaw.
   9|             → We continued work on the restoration of the Arnold Litch family burial plot, which is a joint project with the
  10|                   Cemetery Commission. The DPW authorized a ground radar search and located the lost graves of the desecrated
  11|                   cemetery. A bronze plaque acknowledging the Historical and Cemetery Commissions was purchased and will be
  12|                   placed at the Arnold/Litch Cemetery plot.
  13|             → Work continued on the Levi Goodrich family burial plot. This is another joint project with the Cemetery
  14|                   Commission. Meetings were held on site with the DW, Cemetery Dept. and Cemetery Commission. The plot and
  15|                  the plan was reviewed. The DPW authorized ground radar and a number of unmarked children's graves were
  16|                   discovered. The cemetery had been neglected and desecrated, and the gravestones had been toppled. Some
  17|                   gravestones were broken and lost. Levi Goodrich's headstone was fragmented and deteriorated with some pieces
  18|                   being lost. The Commission is recreating a replacement stone for this grave.
  19|              → The Commission explored and voted to move forward with a veteran's banner program in the APD. It is a proposed
  20|                  joint project with the APDC. Many communities have instituted such programs, which honors veterans by putting
  21|                   banners on telephone poles with the name and photograph of a local veteran on it.
  22|             → The Historical Commission has started research on the history of African American slaves and Freedmen in
  23|                   Lunenburg.
  24|              → We supported the APDC in its work to advocate for and protect the historic buildings and houses located within the
  25|                   National Historic District.

===PAGE 66=== (ocr, 46 lines)
   1|                                                        Lunenburg Public Library
   2|                             "Whatever the cost of our libraries, the price is cheap compared to that of an ignorant
   3|                             nation."-Walter Cronkite
   4|             As the pandemic stretched into Fiscal Year 2022, we saw lingering disruption of old patterns and new patterns of
   5|             behavior continuing. One constant, however, seems to repeat itself: the library builds value for the community.
   6|             The library has continued to follow its strategic plan this year. Keeping our mission close to our hearts:
   7|                        Lunenburg Public Library welcomes all and serves residents of the Lunenburg area as a hub for the community,
   8|                        enriching our lives through its collections, programs, services, facility, and people.
   9|             Our library resources reach far into the community. You no longer need to visit the library in person to access our
  10|             resources. The library has focused on improving and expanding the number of resources available digitally, and made
  11|             concerted efforts to ease access to those resources. We also added Niche Academy to help patrons learn about our digital
  12|             resources and how to access them. Another new resource that will be much appreciated is Mango Languages, offering a
  13|             convenient way to learn over 70 foreign languages, with a focus on conversations.
  14|             Patrons can use all of our databases, e-books and e-audiobooks, our website (lunenburglibrary.org), and even streaming
  15|             services like Kanopy from their homes or cars or workplace. You could even attend a virtual program or interact with
  16|             library staff for help on a question or a reading recommendation without visiting the library. Of course, if you never
  17|             visited, you would be missing out on the beautiful building.
  18|             You would also be missing out on one of the most important services we offer: library programs. Although attendance in
  19|             -person during FY22 was not as consistent as it was immediately before the pandemic, there are still programs
  20|             happening at the library nearly every day, and our consistency paid dividends during the summer, as attendance at
  21|             programs really returned to a normal level. During the year library statt tocused on providing access to programs
  22|             virtually and in-person, and continuing to build a dependable, consistent calendar of events that would appeal to a variety
  23|             of people.
  24|             Library staff have also focused on trying to find new ways for residents to engage with our library collection.
  25|             Particularly worthy of note are curated experiences that have been very popular. Individuals even travel from nearby
  26|             communities to try out these kits. Our Adult Services Librarian, Lydia Gravel, introduced the idea with "Mystery Travel
  27|             Kits" which create an enriching learning experience mimicking some parts of travelling to a foreign country by using
  28|             items in the library collection. Each kit typically contains several books, a movie or two, music, and some activities or
  29|             games. These kits are still being curated and can be requested through our online event calendar on our website.
  30|             As the year went on, we saw more and more people actually visiting the library. Each afternoon during the school year,
  31|             we have been seeing more students than ever before. Partnering with the Boys and Girls Club brings dozens of students
  32|             to study during the afternoon Power Hour. In addition to that, we often see dozens of other teens, spending time at the
  33|             library. They play video games, study quietly, eat snacks, and sometimes make a little too much noise. We are, however,
  34|             excited that they have chosen to come to the library after school.
  35|             We continue to focus on stewarding the facility for the future. Efficiency and sustainability are priorities for the trustees
  36|             and the director. This year we completed replacement of a number of lighting fixtures and bulbs, so that we are now
  37|             almost completely lit by LEDs. The HVAC system has also been at the forefront of our attention, as it is beginning to
  38|             show the signs of its age. Increased maintenance needs are typical of an aging system, and ours is no exception. We will
  39|             continue to make responsible plans for the facility, so that it will be a gem for Lunenburg for decades to come.
  40|             Adult Services Notes
  41|             • We launched 4 reading challenges for adults, using Beanstack logging software to record:
  42|                                     • 66 readers who joined in at least one challenge, reading more than 245,733 minutes
  43|                                         • 683 badges were earned for specific goals or milestones within the challenges
  44|             • We hosted 105 programs and meetings for our adult patrons and 1,040 people attended.
  45|                                         • 13 of these events were held virtually, mostly on the Zoom meeting platform.
  46|                                                                                               66

   1|             LUNENBURG PUBLIC ACCESS (PAC)
   2|             FY22 Mission: Establish, develop, modernize and operate the Town of Lunenburg's
   3|             Public, Government, and Educational (PEG) Access Channels to reflect what matters to
   4|             Lunenburg
   5|             Public Access Cable Committee (PACC)
   6|             Steve Walker, Chair
   7|             Pete Lincoln, Vice Chair
   8|             Steve Archambault, Secretary
   9|             Faith Beall, Member
  10|             Paul Sarapas, Member
  11|             The Lunenburg Public Access Cable Committee (PACC) is responsible for the long-term plan to manage the Public
  12|              Access Channels available to the Lunenburg community through Comcast, the town's only cable-service provider.
  13|             The overall strategy includes providing and improving equipment to acquire, record, broadcast and re-broadcast
  14|             programming from both town, school functions and meetings, as well as programming from publicly available sources.
  15|             Programming
  16|             CH9 - Public
  17|             Public Access Cable (PAC) produced 63% of the local programming on Ch9 in FY22, down 7% from FY21. The total
  18|             number of Public (Ch9) programs, both local and AccessShare (outside resources), decreased from 91 in FY21 to 82
  19|             programs in FY22.
  20|             The 52 local programs included high school sports, band concerts, school events and featured projects. The remaining 30
  21|             (37%) programs were obtained through shared media sources.
  22|             Channel 9 - Public Channel
  23|                                                                                                   37%
  24|                                                                                                   Other
  25|                                                                                 63%              Sources
  26|                                                                                Local
  27|             Several categories of local programming were aired on Ch9. Among the top programs were Informational 28%, Events
  28|             23% and Entertainment 22%, making up the majority 63% of Ch9 programming (see chart, below).
  29|             Two of the top three program categories, Informational and Events (51%) provided viewers with presentations and
  30|             overviews that helped to familiarize them with resources and information, as well as current events happening around
  31|             town.

  11|             (massaccess.telvue.com), include Massachusetts State Agency PSAs and programs of a political nature. There were also
  12|             additional programs from other access stations added, including the Kushmeric Connection from Fitchburg Public
  13|             FY22 brought a slight increase in Government programming with more committee meeting and event recording requests.
  14|             The additional programming allowed residents to view meetings they normally wouldn't see. And during this time, PAC
  15|             also improved the recording of Town Meeting using 4 cameras, which increased the overall quality of the broadcast.
  16|             A few committees and boards continue to use Zoom only, (not live broadcast) and record meetings to the Cloud. These
  17|             meetings are uploaded from the Cloud to one of the Lunenburg Public Access YouTube channels (LunenburgAccess) for
  18|             later viewing. This has resulted in easy access to a greater number of board and committee meetings.

  23|             "Fur, Fins and Feathers", and "Chess Chat."

   1|             stations mostly located in Massachusetts but reaching as far as California. We are delighted to showcase our programs
   2|             to the community while using the database to add to PAC content.
   3|             In addition to MassAccess Telvue downloads, PAC also receives local programs from nearby access stations that air on

   5|             PAC Manager, staff and committee members regularly attend MassAccess conferences and Meet & Greets to develop
   6|             relationships and resources with other media centers.
   7|             Studio and Equipment
   8|             PAC has had studio space in the TC Passios school since 2013. We encourage the public to take advantage of the
   9|             facilities and equipment offered for creative productions. The PAC manager also supports various town departments in
  10|             the use of public access' resources for their communication needs.
  11|             In FY22 PAC made a major investment adding new studio set lighting. With the addition of professional lighting,
  12|             programs saw a drastic increase in quality. PAC videographers were trained on the use of the new lighting and have the
  13|             experience and know-how to provide professional quality programming.
  14|             As the need for multi-camera productions has increased, PAC added a new AV MATRIX portable switcher. This
  15|             switcher was used on location for Town Meetings as well as sports and events. It replaced the outdated switcher which
  16|             was complicated and cumbersome.
  17|             Staff, Producers and Volunteers
  18|             In FY22, there was one full-time Manager and 5 part-time contract videographers and a contracted technical consultant.
  19|             There were openings for 2 part-time videographers.
  20|             The PAC Manager administers the day-to-day operation of the organization, including all production and programming
  21|             schedules, staff scheduling, equipment and services procurement, studio and inventory administration, advertising, social
  22|             media and equipment and system troubleshooting. Videographers provided video recording of committee meetings and
  23|             additional local video assignments and projects.
  24|             A technical contract consultant worked with the PAC manager to provided technical and training assistance as needed.
  25|             It is expected that this contract will continue through FY23. However, PAC searched for, and has the funds budgeted to
  26|             hire a permanent part-time Production Technician to add to the staff in FY23.
  27|             PAC is always looking for producers. If a community member has a special skill or interest, PAC will help make the
  28|             show happen. Our studio and staff are equipped to assist new producers in video production.
  29|             Much effort has gone into recruiting volunteers, with little success.
  30|             Financials
  31|             PAC received its income from Comcast in quarterly payments (received August, November, February and May) and
  32|             monthly interest payments deposited into the Enterprise Fund. The overall revenue received for FY22 from Comcast was
  33|             $199,956.37 staying flat from $199,736.30 (+0.11 % over FY21). The budget starting balance was set at $220,866 for
  34|             FY22 projected expenses and capital costs.

   1|              Summary
   2|              FY22 was a recovery year coming out of the pandemic of FY20 and FY21. The new "normal" has become standard
   3|              practice. Zoom Hybrid meetings continue to be administered with new on-location Zoom Hybrid environments being
   4|              planned for boards and committees. PAC continued to administer these meetings and has adapted to the new
   5|             technology. Hybrid meetings have allowed PAC to air more Government meetings than ever before.
   6|              While overall programming did not increase over FY21, PAC made a few key equipment improvements during this
   7|              period. The addition of a new camera switcher made an impact on the production of on-location broadcasts and multi-
   8|              camera shoots. Sports and events were improved due to the streamlined switcher. And in the studio, the addition of new
   9|              lighting has been a "game-changer" bringing our programs to a more professional level. These upgrades will pave the
  10|              way for better productions and content in FY23.
  11|              Won't you join us as a producer or volunteer? PAC's supportive staff offers production assistance,
  12|              making it easy to produce your own show. Our community is thriving with talented and creative individuals - now is
  13|             your time to shine! If producing doesn't appeal to you, then consider "behind the camera" options. Become a volunteer.
  14|              Volunteers are the "life blood" of media centers around the state, and without that involvement PAC will not be able to
  15|             grow. Consider volunteering to help provide the necessary resources needed for PAC's continued success.
  16|              We need you!
  17|                                              SENIOR PROPERTY TAX WORK-OFF PROGRAM
  18|                                                                  JANUARY 1, 2022 - NOVEMBER 1, 2022
  19|              The Senior Property Tax Work-Off Committee is administered by the Assistant Town Manager/HR Director.
  20|              What is the Senior Property Tax Work-Off!
  21|              The Senior Property Tax Work-Off Program offers income eligible senior citizens age 60+ an opportunity to have an
  22|                batement applied to their property tax bill. Participants work in various town departments doing jobs such as gener
  23|                ffice work, custodial, library shelving, meals on wheels, school assistance, etc., to earn the tax abatemen
  24|             The 2022 tax abatement maximum is $1,500 and applied to the participant's 3" quarter real estate tax bill. The current
  25|              hourly rate is $15.00 per hour.
  26|             Eligibility Requirements
  27|             • Applicants must be 60 years of age or older
  28|             • Must occupy and be the owner of the property as of July 1st
  29|             • Receive a property tax bill in their name or their spouse's name (60 years+)
  30|             • Meet the income guidelines of (proof of income is required):
  31|                        one person household - $50,000
  32|                       two person household - $75,000
  33|              • Earnings are exempt from state taxes
  34|             • Earnings are subject to OBRA if participants are not receiving a pension from either Worcester Regional Retirement
  35|                   or Massachusetts State Teachers Retirement
  36|             • Final amount earned will be applied to the 3"d quarter real estate tax
  37|             • All new applicants are required to have a CORI background check
  38|             • All Property Tax Workers must re-apply and be accepted annually for participation in the program
  39|             2022 Participation
  40|              During 2022, there were eight residents who participated in the Program in the following departments:
  41|             • Building Department/Zoning
  42|                                                                                               73

  12|                                                                  SEWER COMMISSION - FY22
  13|             The Sewer Commission bears the responsibility of overseeing the Sewer Enterprise Fund, planning for the future, and
  14|             developing and implementing rules, regulations and policies. Under the Town's Charter, responsibility for the day-to-
  15|             day operation, care and maintenance of the sanitary sewers are under the supervision of the Town Manager who
  16|             delegates the responsibility to manage and maintain the operational side of the system to the Department of Public
  17|             Works Director. The Town of Lunenburg does not operate a sewer treatment plant. Approximately 86% of Lunenburg
  18|             sewer is discharged to the City of Leominster, and the other 14% is discharged to the City of Fitchburg.
  19|             In FY22 the following members served on the Sewer Commission; Dave MacDonald-Chair, John Reynolds-Vice Chair,
  20|             Brett Ramsden-Clerk and Members Mike Deveikis and Mike Nault. The Commission continued to meet virtually via
  21|             Zoom for all meetings and voted to begin each meeting with the Pledge of Allegiance.
  22|             There are currently 915 properties connected to the Lunenburg system and another 327 which have access to Town
  23|             Sewer but are not yet connected. During FY22, 14 new accounts connected to Town Sewer. The Commission billed out
  24|             $1,021,525 in quarterly sewer bills and had $30,593 of prior year delinquencies added to the owners' property tax bills
  25|             for non-payment.
  26|             During FY22, CTE (Chad Empey) continued to provide excellent service for the Operation & Maintenance of the Sewer
  27|             Stations. The Commission approved funds for repairs to the Dana Street Station which included the replacement of the
  28|             impellers, seals and gaskets and approved the replacement of the electrical panel at the Mass 2 station. These upgrades
  29|             are important to keep the stations running smoothly. The Commission voted 3/8/22 to approve the Year 3 contract with
  30|             CTE, with a 4% increase to commence 7/1/22.
  31|             DPW Director Rob Oliva worked with the Commission in FY22 to implement the Maintenance Plan for the sewer
  32|             system. Clean Basins was contracted to camera and auger a blockage on Mass Ave. near Riley Road. In October 2021
  33|             Green Mountain Pipe grouted the pipe joints on Elizabeth St. to address a long-standing Inflow/Infiltration (I/I) issue in
  34|             3 services. Rob unfortunately left Lunenburg in March 2022, but the Commission welcomed Bill Bernard in May as
  35|             Director.
  36|                                                                                               74

===PAGE 75=== (ocr, 39 lines)
   1|              SEWER COMMISSION - FY22
   2|              Bill has been working diligently with the Commission to carry out the many Maintenance Plan projects in process, such
   3|              as jetting, manhole repairs and overseeing maintenance at the pump stations.
   4|              In March 2022 the Commission approved hiring an Assistant Business Manager, to share the increasing demands of the
   5|               Business Manager's job. Paula Bertram was hired to assist the Business Manager, Barb Lefebvre with billing, account
   6|              management and other office activities.
   7|              In FY22, the Commission continued the annual permit program for the 20 Food Service Establishments with grease traps
   8|              and interceptors on Town Sewer. The Commission once again voted to waive the Annual Permit Renewal Fee for the
   9|               Grease Trap Program, as many restaurants were still struggling to gain back business after the Covid 19 closures. In
  10|              February 2022, the Commission voted to drop the Annual Permit Renewal fees for establishments in compliance,
  11|              effective with FY23 renewals. Those who are not in compliance will pay $350 for internal traps and $650 for external
  12|              trap renewals. Grease Trap compliance continues to be a focus of the Commission as Fats, Oils and Grease (FOG) can
  13|              negatively affect the smooth operation and maintenance costs of the sewer system. While the Grease Trap Regulation
  14|              focuses on commercial and public disposal, individual homeowners can also contribute a significant amount of grease
  15|              into the system which increases everyone's costs. Whether on sewer or septic, grease should not be put down the drain.
  16|              While working on the FY23 budget, an 8% rate increase was approved by the Commission to cover increasing costs in
  17|              usage from Fitchburg and Leominster. The rate increased from $12.65/100 cu.ft. to $13.66/100 cu.ft. and the minimum
  18|              charge from $107.00 to $115.00/quarter. The Commission continued working in FY22 with Flow Assessment to
  19|              maintain and ensure the accuracy of the flow meters that measure usage going to Fitchburg and Leominster.
  20|              The Commission also voted to update several policies for re-connection fees, prorating bills for change of ownership or
  21|              new connections, easements and waivers. A public hearing will be held in FY23 to address the policy updates.
  22|              The Commission met with several groups in FY22 looking to expand sewer. The Parks Commission requested to expand
  23|              the Sewer Service Area (SSA) to include Marshall Park, which was approved at the November 2021 Town Meeting.
  24|              Extensions for Upland Ave./Eastern Ave. and White St./Maple Parkway were discussed and preliminary designs, with
  25|              estimates, were done by Wright Pierce. However, a survey of residents did not indicate the necessary 2/3rds interest in
  26|              favor of the extensions. Other projects before the Commission were an 8-unit condo at 711 Mass. Ave., lots at 790-792
  27|              Mass. Ave., and a marijuana establishment (Uma Flowers) at 43 Summer St. A horse rescue facility and 20-unit
  28|              residential project at 5 Chestnut St. requested to be added to the SSA. An article was added to the May Town Meeting
  29|              but was passed over. The 200 apartment 40B project application on Howard Street was approved by Mass Housing to
  30|               proceed in seeking approvals from the Lunenburg ZBA. The project will seek a large expansion of the Sewer Service
  31|               Area and Water District to bring utilities to the project.
  32|              At the 5/7/22 Town Meeting, the Commission put forward 4 articles, 3 of which related to the FY23 budget and
  33|              stabilization funds. For the standard budget article for FY23, $853,112.53 was appropriated from Sewer Enterprise
  34|              revenues, $20,000.00 transferred from the Sewer Capital Reserve Stabilization Fund, $20,962.00 was transferred from
  35|              the Sewer Reserve Capacity Fund and the sum of $319,107.47 was appropriated from Retained Earnings for a total
  36|              appropriation of $1,213,182.00 to operate the Sewer Enterprise Fund for FY23, including the amount of $793,968.47
  37|              approved to cover both the Enterprise Fund's share of Town services and debt service on the sewer betterments. The
  38|              second article transferred monies to the Capital Reserve Stabilization Fund in the amount of $35,548.86, approximately
  39|                                                                                               75

===PAGE 77=== (ocr, 44 lines)
   1|                                                                           VETERANS' SERVICES
   2|             The Commonwealth of Massachusetts has the most comprehensive system of assistance offered to Veterans,
   3|             families, and surviving spouses in all of the United States. Every city or town in Massachusetts, in accordance
   4|             with chapter 115 of the Massachusetts General Laws, must have available to its citizens a Veterans' Services
   5|             Officer (VSO), either full- or part-time, as determined by the community population, in order to serve the
   6|             needs of its Veterans. Lunenburg has a part-time VSO at the Ritter Building at 960 Massachusetts Ave.
   7|             Working with the Town census of 2021, shows Lunenburg in excess of 618 residents who claimed either
   8|             military service or veteran status. On a monthly basis, the veterans and widows of veterans in Lunenburg
   9|             receive in excess of $328,330.74 in federal benefits (Veterans Administration), which they mostly put back
  10|             into the community by paying their taxes and through discretionary spending.
  11|             Veterans, families, or widows who quality can be provided monthly financial assistance (reimbursements) for
  12|             specitic living expenses such as heating fuel, medical care, and pharmaceutical co-payments. With the VSO's
  13|             approval, the Town of Lunenburg provides the financial assistance and is then reimbursed by the state's
  14|             department of Veterans' Services (DVS). It is the function of the Veterans' Services Office to identify
  15|             qualifying members of the community and assist with enrollment into the DVS program. Additionally, certain
  16|             Veterans and/or widows may also be eligible for federal benefits from the Veterans Administration (VA).
  17|             Only Massachusetts offers both of these services to every community.
  18|             During Fiscal Year 2022 (July 2021- June 2022), Lunenburg served an average of 10 recipients (Veterans,
  19|             families, or widows) per month. Lunenburg paid out $64,435.43 in benefits, or an average of $5,369.62 per
  20|             recipient per month. Lunenburg was reimbursed $48,326.57 by DVS, which is 75% of what was given as
  21|             assistance, the maximum allowable share.
  22|             Some examples of Veterans' Service Office assistance services include:
  23|                Enrollment in chapter 115, State Veterans benefits
  24|                   Assistance obtaining military service records or replacement medals/decorations
  25|             •     Tuition waivers at Massachusetts state colleges
  26|                  Housing assistance, including Veterans Shelters and Veterans Homes
  27|             •  Payments of "Welcome Home" bonuses for deployed Veterans
  28|                   Assistance obtaining certain Massachusetts automobile and motorcycle license plates
  29|            • Employment assistance
  30|             The Veterans' Service Office also provides information, advice, and assistance to any citizen interested in
  31|             learning about their qualifications for federal benefits, such as:
  32|             •  VA disability payments and property tax exemptions
  33|             • Tax-exempt annuities for parents or spouses of those killed in Action or those who are declared 100%
  34|                  disabled from service-connected conditions.
  35|            •   VA pensions, Aid and Attendance assistance, and death benefits
  36|             • VA medical care for both physical and mental needs
  37|                   Vocational and Educational opportunities (the GI Bill)
  38|                   VA Loans
  39|             • Burial Assistance and Grave Markers (Veterans or Civilian cemeteries)
  40|             Respectfully submitted,
  41|             David Lauzon Jr.
  42|             Interim VSO
  43|             Office phone 978-582-4176

   1|             Community Health- Communications and Public Health Education Summary
   2|             The Public Health Educator/Communications Specialist (PHE/CS) role has evolved since its inception in Sept 2021 to
   3|             best support the needs of the District. In the past year, the PHE/CS has been communicating timely and relevant public
   4|             health information to towns and stakeholders, and providing educational opportunities across the District with a social
   5|             determinants of health (SDOH) lens. This work has included:
   6|             • Developing educational programs and presentations both independently and collaboratively with the PH nursing
   7|                   team- Harm reduction, emergency preparedness, etc.
   8|             • Providing education and advocacy related to treatment and de-stigmatization of substance use disorder
   9|             • Maintaining up-to-date distribution lists, communicating within the district via channels such as quarterly
  10|                  newsletters, regular email updates, etc. pertaining to public health news, information and events such as flu and
  11|                  rabies clinics. Exploring ways to diversify communication methods to reach as many community members as
  12|                   possible.
  13|             • Building and maintaining relationships with stakeholders through meetings and attending community events to
  14|                  represent NABH
  15|             • Serving as a resource for community members with public health related questions across a broad range of topics;
  16|                  developing a robust, in-depth Resources page for the website (which is currently being finalized)
  17|             • Attending trainings with an emphasis on health equity and public health service regionalization. MDPH has
  18|                  prioritized these areas because research has shown they have the biggest impact on health outcomes for the broadest
  19|                  range of people.
  20|             • PHE/CS has utilized an SDOH lens to identify health inequities that most impact the NABH region to focus efforts
  21|                  and collaborate with community partners to lessen these gaps.
  22|             • Developing educational and communications materials for district and individual towns by request including print,
  23|                   electronic, presentation, and TV/news media materials
  24|             • Maintaining and improving upon NABH website to keep info relevant and accessible. Maintaining agency social
  25|                  media to reach more people in district with current, relevant and seasonal public health information.
  26|             • Organizing events to facilitate the exchange of information such as a Spring Wellness Festival and first regional in
  27|                  formal local board of health gathering

===PAGE 84=== (ocr, 52 lines)
   1|                                                                           FIRE DEPARTMENT
   2|              The Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians of the Lunenburg Fire Department are dedicated to providing
   3|              the highest quality, most cost effective fire and emergency medical services to the citizens of the town of Lunenburg.
   4|                        In 2022, your Fire Department responded to 1990 calls for service, broken down as follows:
   5|                        Fires, Alarms & Hazardous Conditions                                           385                    Emergency Medical 1143
   6|                        Inspections
   7|             This represents a 14% increase in emergency calls for service over 2021, mostly in Emergency Medical calls.
   8|                        The Department consists of 10 Career and 30-35 On Call/Per Diem Firefighters and Emergency Medical
   9|             Technicians, plus a full time Administrative Assistant/EMS Coordinator. Three Firefighter/EMT or Paramedics are on
  10|             duty 24/7 at the Station. This coverage is provided by a combination of our Career Staff and Per Diem Firefighters.
  11|             Staffing the Station with dual trained Fire/EMS personnel ensures that we are ready to respond to whatever emergency
  12|             occurs. As many calls require more personnel than are on duty, we continue to rely heavily on the dedication and
  13|             commitment of our On Call Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians.
  14|                        After nearly five years of preparation, the Department is in the final stages of licensing to provide our own
  15|             Paramedic Service. Firefighter Olivia Hayes certified as a Paramedic in October 2022 and Firefighter Melissa Buchanan
  16|             will be obtaining her certification in January 2023. This brings us to four Fulltime Firefighter/Paramedics with an
  17|             additional eight On Call/per diem Firefighter/Paramedics. In addition, Firefighter Jennie Smith will begin training as a
  18|             Paramedic in January of 2023. Paramedic training is in excess of 2000 hours and requires anywhere from 9-18 months
  19|             of college level classroom education, clinical time and field internships to obtain certification.
  20|             continues to train our newly hired full time staff to this level and recruit licensed Paramedics to fill Career positions
  21|             when possible. We will begin providing Paramedic Level care to the community in January 2023.
  22|                        In 2022, the Fire Prevention Division conducted 446 Inspections. This is in addition to multiple fire drills and
  23|             public education initiatives. By law, we are required to inspect Smoke and Carbon Monoxide Detector installations
  24|             when a home is sold. We are also required to inspect all new oil burner and propane gas installations. Any business
  25|             holding a liquor license is also requires a yearly inspection for fire safety issues. In addition, all new construction
  26|             projects must have their plans reviewed for proper fire protection, as well as ongoing inspections during the construction
  27|             process. Other Fire Safety Initiatives undertaken during 2022 include:
  28|                  Continuation of a Department of Fire Services Grant in the amount of $6953 to assist with Fire Safety Education in
  29|                   both the Schools and with Senior Citizens. This Grant provides funding for both materials and personnel to bring
  30|                   fire safety education to our most vulnerable populations.
  31|                   Participation in the Senior Center's "Sand for Seniors" Program. This program provides buckets of walkway sand
  32|                   for Senior Citizens who were unable to obtain it for themselves in the winter months. In partnership with this
  33|                  program, Captain James Ricci delivers the sand to the residents and when possible, inspects their homes for Smoke
  34|                  and Carbon Monoxide Detectors. If the resident was lacking detectors, Capt. Ricci is able to install free detectors
  35|                  obtained through the Senior SAFE Grant.
  36|                 It should be noted that each year, 50% of fatal fires are found to have nonfunctional or nonexistent smoke detectors.
  37|                                    Firefighters Samuel Johnson and Timothy O'Connell graduated from the Massachusetts Firefighting
  38|             Academy's Call/Volunteer Recruit Training Program in June of 2022. This 240-hour program trains new personnel in
  39|             the basics of firefighting and hazardous materials response. The program is a requirement for any new Firefighters with
  40|             the Department.
  41|                        Eight Firefighters from Lunenburg continue to be members of the Fire District 8 Technical Rescue and Dive
  42|             Teams. These regional teams respond to high risk and unusual rescue situations, such as Confined Space, Rescue from
  43|             Heights, Building and Trench Collapse, Swift/Floodwater and Underwater Search and Recovery. In conjunction with
  44|             these Teams, Lunenburg operates both a Water Rescue Unit and a Collapse/Trench Rescue Trailer.
  45|                         Apparatus replacement has reached a plateau for a few years, with two pumpers and the Ladder truck all under
  46|             10 years old. The Ambulances continue to see increased wear and tear as we see not only increased calls for service, but
  47|             longer transport times to other hospitals and critical care facilities. It should be noted that 30 years ago, we had four
  48|             hospitals within a 10-15 minute transport time. Today there are two. In addition, many cardiac and trauma patients
  49|             require definitive treatment in specialty facilities, often requiring transports to Worcester. Given long delays in
  50|             obtaining new units, a replacement for Rescue 1, our 2014 ambulance has been moved up two years and is being
  51|             requested on the FY'24 Capital Plan.
  52|                                                                                               84

===PAGE 85=== (ocr, 29 lines)
   1|                                                                           FIRE DEPARTMENT
   2|                        The Turnout Gear Replacement program that was begun under the Capital Budget last year has been completed.
   3|             All personnel now have state of the art protective clothing, with a serviceable spare set of equipment as well.
   4|             Firefighting protective clothing must be cleaned after every fire to rid it of cancer causing materials that are often
   5|             byproducts of today's synthetic materials burning. As washing and drying firefighting cloth can take from 24-48 hours,
   6|             having a spare set available allows all personnel to remain "in service" at all time.
   7|                        Replacement of our outdated Forest Fire protective clothing with a dual-purpose type of clothing that is suitable
   8|             for both brush fires and motor vehicle/technical rescue situations is still underway. This project is funded through a
   9|             State Grant for Safety Equipment.                Though supply chain issues have slowed the process, we anticipate having the
  10|             equipment by spring of 2023. A State Firefighting Safety Grant was also received in 2022. This will replace several
  11|             hundred feet of fire hose and some outdated nozzles and adapters. This grant is expected to be completed sometime in
  12|             2023.
  13|                        In conclusion, I would like to thank the members of the Lunenburg Fire Department for their outstanding
  14|             commitment to the Town. Their enthusiasm, professionalism and dedication continue to impress me every day. In
  15|             addition, I would like to express my gratitude to the members of the Department who have helped make the Paramedic
  16|             Program a reality. From those who helped develop policies and those two helped procure equipment, to those members
  17|             who endured the challenges of Paramedic training.                          Your efforts have helped bring about a massive improvement in
  18|             service to our residents. My thanks also to the Town Manager for her unwavering support of this vital project.
  19|             public safety. Such support allows us to continue providing the high level of service you have come to expect from us.

   8|                   Mission Statement:
   9|                   We, the members of the Lunenburg Police Department, strive to provide the highest quality of police services
  10|                  to our community members and visitors alike. We believe in Community Policing, and all of the benefits that
  11|                  are realized from this concept. We will strive to maintain the strong partnerships that have been developed
  12|                   with our community, and continuously look for ways improve that relationship. We will strive to deliver
  13|                   police services in a tair and impartial manner, consistent with all applicable laws, while respecting all
  14|                  individual rights. We will use innovative and modern ideas and techniques to solve crimes, apprehend
  15|                   criminal offenders, and help resolve quality of life issues.
  16|                  Values:
  17|             • We are committed to ensuring that all of our actions reflect the utmost integrity, and honesty.
  18|             • We are committed to respecting individual rights, human dignity, and the values of all members of the
  19|                  community and members of the Lunenburg Police Department.
  20|             • We are a proactive and problem solving agency.
  21|             • We strive to serve our citizens in a professional manner with compassion, respect, and empathy.
  22|             • We are committed to providing the highest quality of police services to our community.
  23|             • We recognize the value of our employees, and recognize they are our most valued resource. We will strive to
  24|                  provide our employees with the necessary resources, training, and career development to enhance their
  25|                   professionalism.

   6|             Department Review:
   7|             It is an honor to again provide the Lunenburg Police Department's Annual report as the Chief of Police. 2022 continued
   8|             to be a challenging year for the Police Department as the community came out of the grips of Covid-19. We have also
   9|             faced the challenges associated with the economy, population growth, and increased traffic within the community. A
  10|             nationwide trend of recruiting and retaining Police Officers continue to have an effect on our Department. Although
  11|             faced with these challenges, the Department remains committed to being pro-active, and strives to make the Community
  12|             safe for all.
  13|             K9 Jerry will be retiring at the end of the fiscal year. Jerry has been with the Department since 2014, and has performed
  14|             admirably throughout his career. Jerry will remain with Sgt. Tocci, who has agreed to keep him through his retirement
  15|             years. We wish Jerry good health and a long retirement. Job well done Jerry.
  16|             Off. Nicholas Marino accepted a position with the Acton Police Department. We thank Nick for his service, and wish
  17|             him the best of luck with Acton PD.
  18|             Reserve Officer/Desk Officer Lisa Melnicki has accepted a position in the private sector. We thank Lisa for her service,
  19|             and wish her the best of luck in her future endeavors.
  20|             Off. Jesse Reed was hired to the full times ranks. We are happy to welcome a veteran police officer from Berlin Police
  21|             Department who will bring his experience and knowledge to the Department.
  22|             Off. Deven O'Brien has returned to the Department as a full time police officer. Off. O'Brien started his career with our
  23|             Department as a Reserve Officer in 2013 and in 2014 was appointed as a full time police officer. In 2018 Off. O'Brien
  24|             accepted a position with the Marlborough Police Department. We were happy to welcome Off. O'Brien back to the
  25|             Department, and continue his service where his Law Enforcement career began.
  26|             Off. Paul Theodoulou was hired to the full time ranks after serving a short period time as a Reserve Officer with the
  27|             Department. Off. Theodoulou is a veteran police officer from Fitchburg Police Department as well Townsend Police
  28|             Department. Off. Theodoulou brings a wealth of experience and knowledge to our Department. We were fortunate to
  29|             have landed Off. Theodoulou.
  30|             Off. George "Trey" Tibbetts was elevated to a full time position after serving a short period of time as a Reserve Officer
  31|             with the Department. Off. Tibbetts was added to the Department in accordance with the Department's 10 year hiring
  32|             plan. Trey completed his training in a relatively short period of time, and we are excited to watch him grow with the
  33|             Department.
  34|             As we became fully staffed we anticipated filling our vacant positions. The first and foremost was filling a vacant
  35|             Sergeant's position that would provide us much needed supervision for the Patrol Bureau. We also anticipated filling a
  36|             vacant Detective's position, as well as adding an additional School Resource/Community Outreach Officer.
  37|             Sgt. John Morreale was promoted to fill the vacant Sergeant's position. Sgt. Morreale has been with the Department
  38|             since 2014 when he began his career as a Reserve Officer, and has worked up through the ranks as he has progressed in
  39|             his career. Sgt. Morreale has been assigned as the Department's Accreditation Manager as well as the split shift patrol
  40|             supervisor.
  41|             Det. Joshua Mathieu was elevated to the Detective position. Det. Mathieu started with the Department in 2011 and has
  42|             risen through the ranks to his current position. Det. Mathieu brings a wealth of experience, knowledge and training to the
  43|             position. Det. Mathieu is an excellent investigator, and will be a great addition to the Investigative Bureau.
  44|             On October 26, 2022 the Department was certified through the Massachusetts Police Accreditation Commission
  45|             (MPAC), which is recognized as a best practice organization. Through the tireless work of Sgt. Morreale, and the
  46|             cooperation and collaboration with members of our Department we were able to complete the first phase of
  47|             accreditation. The Department was recognized and received the MPAC award on October 26, 2022 at the Commission's
  48|                                                                                               88

===PAGE 89=== (ocr, 39 lines)
   1|               award ceremony held that Marlborough Country Club. We look forward to an accelerated process and becoming fully
   2|                accredited by the summer of 2023.
   3|                We continue to work towards a Co-Response Model approach to mental health type of calls. This would involve
   4|               partnering officer(s) with a mental health clinician to assist with individuals in crisis. We have been meeting with
   5|               Community Health Link and Advocates to assess our needs, and develop a Co-Response plan. We have also been
   6|               exploring a partnership with the Town of Lancaster to share a clinician between the towns. We hope to have this
   7|               program become a reality by the fall of 2023.

  10|               addressed, and provide a safe community to live, work and visit. The Department evaluates effectiveness, in part,
  11|               by statistical information that is generated through the Records Management System.
  12|               The following statistical information is to provide some insight into our officers' activity for 2022.

===PAGE 90=== (ocr, 55 lines)
   1|                As result of the above calls our ofticers generated a total of 1,777 written reports. There were 434 reports
   2|               involving Incident Based Reporting (IBR), and National Incident Based Reporting (NIBRS), which is a State and
   3|               Federal Crime reporting data base. The Officers also generated an additional 483 written reports that did not fall
   4|               into the IBR/NIBRS category.
   5|               According to NIBRS, reported crimes are broken down into three categories, Crimes Against Persons, Crimes Against
   6|               Property, and Crimes Against Society. These are further broken down into Group A and Group B categories to closely
   7|               match the Uniform Crime Reporting (UCR) program. The Lunenburg Police Department regularly reports IBR
   8|               information to the State, which in turn reports the information to the Federal Bureau of Investigation.
   9|               In 2022 there has been a 19% increase in Group A, Crimes Against Persons, with Aggravated and Simple Assaults
  10|               as well as Intimidation (witness/victim) accounting for the majority of the incidents with 39 out of 62 total reported
  11|               incidents. These numbers appear to have a direct correlation to the Domestic Violence calls that officers have
  12|               responded to in 2022.
  13|               In 2022 there has been a 34% decrease in Group A, Crimes Against Property. Although we have observed a
  14|                decrease in property crimes, the area we have observed an increase is shoplifting, accounting for a majority of the
  15|               incidents with 40 out of 142 total reported incidents.
  16|             Training:
  17|             Training continues to be a priority for the Department. The Department's in-house trainers look for ingenuitive ways to
  18|             complement our annual in-service training. In addition, several officers have attended specialized training in various
  19|             areas of Law Enforcement as a part of professional development.
  20|             Use of Force:
  21|             The Department conducts an annual analysis of Use of Force incidents, as well as review(s) of the incidents as they
  22|             occur. In 2022 officers were involved in nine (9) use of force incidents, which remained the same as compared to 2021.
  23|             In each Use of Force incident de-escalation tactics, including the use of verbal tactics, were first utilized prior to any Use
  24|             of Force application. Officers continue to strive to de-escalate confrontations prior to any application of Use of Force.
  25|             Motor Vehicle Pursuits:
  26|             The Department conducts an annual analysis of Motor Vehicle Pursuits, as well as review(s) of pursuits as they occur. In
  27|             2022 officers were involved in five (5) motor vehicle pursuits, which remained the same as compared to 2021. One
  28|             pursuit resulted in a motor vehicle accident but occurred after the pursuit had been terminated. No injuries to the public,
  29|             officers or suspects) involved in the pursuits were reported.
  30|             Nashoba Valley Regional Dispatch District:
  31|             The Nashoba Valley Regional Dispatch District (NVRDD) is a Regional Emergency Communications Center located in
  32|             Devens, MA. NVRDD serves as the 9-1-1 public safety answering point and dispatch center for Police, Fire and EMS
  33|             for the communities of Lunenburg, Bolton, Devens, Harvard, Lancaster and Lunenburg. NVRDD was one of the first
  34|             Regional Emergency Communications Centers in the State of Massachusetts, opening its doors on June 18, 2013.
  35|             NVRDD serves a total population of just over 35,000 residents and thousands more visitors to the orchards, farms,
  36|             businesses, and attractions within its six communities. In 2022, NVRDD handled 33,500 phone calls coming in on 911
  37|             and business lines. NVRDD also coordinated the response to over 89,900 calls for service. NVRDD's staff are highly
  38|             skilled in call-handling and dispatching, and are all able to perform both functions simultaneously. These first of the first
  39|             -responders provide a vital and seamless link between the community and their local police, fire, and emergency medical
  40|             services. While the focus is on handling emergencies, our staff also serves the public and our responders by providing
  41|             other administrative and non-emergency dispatch tasks. NVRDD staffs the communications center with multiple
  42|             dispatchers and supervisors each shift to handle calls and requests.
  43|                                                                                                                                                      Town of Lunenburg CFS 2022
  44|             In 2022 NVRDD processed a total of 89,900 calls for service (CFS). Of these calls for service,
  45|             32,067 were for the Town of Lunenburg. These calls for service average out to 2,673 CFS per
  46|             month in the Town of and 88 CFS per day in the Town of Lunenburg. Each CFS represents one
  47|             report entered into the Computer Aided Dispatch system by the dispatchers for the 9-1-1 calls
  48|             handled, the police, fire, or medical emergencies dispatched, and the other miscellaneous duties
  49|             they perform on a daily basis.                                                                                                                    EPOCADHIDEM
  50|             In 2022 NVRDD Telecommunicators answered 10,989 9-1-1 Calls. This total averages out to 916
  51|             emergency 9- 1-1 calls per month and approximately 30 emergency 911 calls per day. NVRDD Staff prides itself in
  52|             customer service and standards of care. Our average 9-1-1 answer time is 3.5 seconds, which is approximately half of a
  53|             normal telephone "ring". Our staff is also evaluated monthly on their call handling skills and they rate at an average 94%
  54|             adherence to the call taking protocols.
  55|                                                                                               90

===PAGE 91=== (ocr, 15 lines)
   1|                NVRDD Telecommunicators average 32 hours of training per year. In 2022 NVRDD welcomed several new
   2|                members to the team: three dispatchers, an administrative coordinator, a treasurer, and an accountant. NVRDD is
   3|                approved for a total of 15 Full-Time Employees, and 3 Part-Time Employees. Currently NVRDD has 14 of the 15
   4|                Full-Time Positions filled, and all 3 Part-Time Positions filled. NVRDD is projected to be fully staffed by the
   5|                beginning of FY24.

   1|                                                                            PLANNING BOARD
   2|             Fourteen (14) ANR Plans (land division plans not requiring approval under the Subdivision Control Law, but still
   3|             requiring Board endorsement) were approved.
   4|             garden, pool, etc. The Board approved stormwater permits for the following:
   5|                        • A warehouse off Leominster-Shirley Road;
   6|                        • A single-family house lot at 324 Flat Hill Road;
   7|                        • Construction of a 7-bay garage at 225 & 259 Leominster-Shirley Road;
   8|                        • The creation of four (4) lots at Cross and Mulpus;
   9|                        • The creation of six (6) lots on the corner of Holman and Northfield Road.
  10|             Lunenburg has four designated Scenic Roads; Lancaster Avenue, Flat Hill Road, Northfield Road and Leominster Road.
  11|             Over the past year you may have noticed building lots, such as some of those mentioned in the paragraph above, being
  12|             created on some of these roads. As these are Scenic Roads, any repair, maintenance, re-construction, or paving work in
  13|             the Town's right-of-way cannot involve the cutting or removal of trees or the tearing down or destruction of stone walls
  14|             without consent of the Planning Board.
  15|             The Commonwealth issued Site Eligibility for a proposed 40B development off Howard Street and the Board provided
  16|             its comments to the Town Manager. The Planning Board in conjunction with many other Boards, Commissions, Town
  17|             Utticials, and private citizens submitted numerous comments outlining the Town's opposition to the development of 200
  18|             apartment units at 400 Howard St. The development of affordable housing is critical to Lunenburg; however, the site in
  19|             question has significant flaws that concern Town Officials. The applicant is currently going through the public hearing
  20|             process with the Zoning Board of Appeals.
  21|             The Board submitted four articles for November 2021 Special Town Meeting.
  22|                  Two of the articles, which passed, updated the zoning bylaw to reflect the current zoning;
  23|             • Two articles were submitted on behalf of the Water District to update the Watershed Protection Map. DEP
  24|                  requested additional changes to the proposed bylaw which Board members were not comfortable with; therefore, the
  25|                   Board chose to pass over the Water District articles.
  26|             The Land Use Director worked on a new draft for 2022 Annual Town Meeting. The two Water District articles were
  27|             submitted for May 2022 town meeting, but did not pass.
  28|             A third article was also submitted for 2022 Annual Town Meeting which added clarification to the permitting process for
  29|             non-medical marijuana establishments. That article passed.
  30|             Uma Flowers, a cannabis retail facility, was awarded a Community Host Agreement from Lunenburg and submitted an
  31|             application for a state-licensed retail establishment under Section 250-4.15. Non-Medical Marijuana Establishments of
  32|             the zoning bylaw. The establishment will be located at 43 Summer Street (site of the former Donnelly's Tavern).
  33|             An application was received for construction of a 367,000 square foot warehouse and distribution building at 260
  34|             Falulah Road. The majority of the building was located on Airport Road/Crawford Street in Fitchburg, but a portion
  35|             extended into Lunenburg.
  36|             Approval was given for the construction of a 372,000 square foot warehouse and distribution building off Leominster-
  37|             Shirley Road near the Shirley town line. As a result of this approval, improvements were made to Leominster-Shirley
  38|             Road with the addition of an eastbound left-turn lane into the project and the widening of the road to the north (towards
  39|             Reservoir Road) on the project side of the street.
  40|             Under the Unitil Reliability Project, Unitil submitted an application for the removal and/or pruning of public shade trees
  41|             along Leominster Road from Sunny Hill Road to Kilburn Street. Some of the proposed trees already had contact with
  42|             existing power lines. Poles would be replaced and lines placed three to seven feet higher than the current placement. By
  43|             upgrading the existing lines Unitil expected to reduce outage time for incidents that occurred from the center of town to
  44|             Goodrich Street.

===PAGE 96=== (ocr, 31 lines)
   1|             The Planning Board requested and was approved for Capital Plan Funding to begin an update of the 2002 Master Plan.
   2|             This update will likely begin in FY23 and carry through FY25. The process will include public visioning sessions, input
   3|             from as many stakeholders as possible, and will aid in moving Lunenburg and its policies through the next decade and
   4|             beyond.
   5|             Legislation passed requiring any MBTA community to have a zoning district of reasonable size in which multi-family
   6|             housing would be permitted as of right. A minimum density of 15 housing units per acre was required. The town is
   7|             currently working on compliance. The results and approach to complying with the requirements outlined in in MGL
   8|             Chapter 40A Section 3A will be done through a public process by the Planning Board.
   9|             The Planning Board created an Economic Development Committee which began meeting in April 2021. Over the past
  10|             year the Committee has worked to build a relationship with the Lunenburg Business Association, the Commonwealth of
  11|             Massachusetts Office of Business Development, and other stakeholders to provide policy recommendations to the
  12|             Planning Board and create an environment that supports existing and proposed business in Lunenburg.
  13|             Meetings resumed at Town Hall with a hybrid model; both in-person and via Zoom.
  14|             Paula Bertram announced she would not seek another term at May 2022 town election. Amanda Reid was elected to the
  15|             Board to serve until 2027.
  16|             The Planning Office is located in the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massachusetts Avenue and can be reached at 978-
  17|             582-4147 extension 5.
