  21|                                                 Ad Hoc Open Space Advisory Committee to the Planning Board
  22|             The Open Space Committee's efforts in FY'22 principally focused on gaining a better understanding of the Town of
  23|             Lunenburg's portfolio of vacant, undeveloped, "general municipal" lands, and assessing which of those properties may
  24|             be repurposed more specifically to meet needs identified in the Town's Open Space and Recreation Plan.
  25|             The Open Space Committee also reviewed several Chapter 61 withdrawal notifications this past year as part of the
  26|             town's defined multi-board process and reviewed the tax sale/taking process in the Spring of 2022.
  27|             The Open Space Committee investigated two parcels on Lake Shirley held by the Town and reviewed them for possible
  28|             lake access (car top kayak and canoe launch) for Lunenburg residents.
  29|             Respectfully submitted,
  30|             Michael Toohill, Chair
  31|                                                                                               96

===PAGE 97=== (ocr, 37 lines)
   1|                                                                            ZONING BOARD OF APPEALS
   2|              The Zoning Board of Appeals heard several petitions during calendar year 2022. There were several applicants seeking
   3|             special permits and special permits for dimensional variations.                           The Zoning Board meetings are held on the second and
   4|             fourth Wednesdays of the month at Town Hall at 7:00PM on an "as needed" basis or ZOOM as an alternative.
   5|             On August 18, 2022 a 40B Comprehensive Permit was filed for property located at 318, 319 & 400 Howard Street, for a
   6|             project known as Pond View Commons owned by The Homes at Aro Estates, LLC.
   7|             The proposal is to construct three multifamily residential buildings under the Massachusetts Comprehensive Permit
   8|             Statute, Massachusetts General Law's c.40B, ss.20-23.
   9|             The applicant Pond View Commons, LLC seeks to create 200 apartment units within three multifamily residential
  10|             buildings.
  11|             The Zoning Board consists of the following members: Alfred Gravelle, Chairman, James Besarkarski, Vice-Chairman,
  12|             Members: Hans Wentrup, David Blatt, Patrick Callahan and Anthony Nicastro and Donald Gurney, Associate members.
  13|             Lisa Normandin serves as the Administrative Assistant to the Zoning Board.
  14|             The functions of your Zoning Board of Appeals as required by statures are as follows:
  15|             1. To hear and decide applications for Variances from Town's Protective By-Laws as authorized in the By-laws.
  16|             2. To hear and decide applications for Special Permits as authorized in the Town's Protective By-laws.
  17|             3. To hear and decide appeals in accordance with Chapter 40A, Section 8 of the Massachusetts General Laws, as
  18|                   amended, from any person aggrieved by reason of his or her inability to obtain a permit or enforcement action from
  19|                  the Administrative Officer or Board in the Town, the Regional Planning Agency, abutting City or Town or other
  20|                   Administrative Official.
  21|             4. To act upon application for comprehensive permits in accordance with Massachusetts General Laws, Chapter 40B.
  22|                   The Zoning Board of Appeals reviews requests for comprehensive permits submitted to the Board under Chapter
  23|                  40B by developers who wish to create affordable housing. These requests can, by law, circumvent local zoning
  24|                   ordinances.
  25|             The office of the Zoning Board of Appeals is located in the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massachusetts Avenue,
  26|             Lunenburg. Business hours are 8:00 AM to 4:00 PM Monday and Wednesday and 8:00 AM to 6:00 PM Tuesday and
  27|             Thursday, closed Friday. I
  28|             f you need any assistance from the Zoning Board of Appeals please contact us at 978-582-4146 X 421 during regular
  29|             business hours. Zoning Board applications are available in the office and online at www.Lunenburgma.gov.

===PAGE 100=== (ocr, 47 lines)
   1|                                                                            Lunenburg Public Schools
   2|                                                                            Superintendent's Message
   3|             I am pleased to present my annual report for the Lunenburg Public Schools. I want to express my gratitude for a
   4|             collaborative approach toward projecting and planning for current and future needs with the Town Manager, Heather
   5|             Lemieux. As the leader of the district, I am fortunate to work with a School Committee that is truly dedicated to serving
   6|             the best interests of our students and appreciative that the Board of Selectmen and Finance Committee work
   7|             collaboratively with the School Committee and School Department to problem-solve in order to better meet the
   8|             educational needs of the children of Lunenburg.
   9|             The district has remained focused on our Strategy for Continuous Improvement, which is aligned with our Mission and
  10|             Vision:
  11|             The Mission of the Lunenburg Public Schools is to provide all students with the skills, confidence, and passion for life-
  12|             long learning so that they may find their own paths to successful careers, active citizenship, and rewarding lives.
  13|             The Lunenburg Public Schools is committed to becoming a leader in education by focusing on its values of attending to
  14|             the personal well-being of its students, providing high-quality curricula and student-centered instruction for deeper
  15|             learning at all levels, and promoting the development of attributes and skills necessary for continuous learning for all.
  16|             The Strategy is a living document. Each August the District Leadership Team reviews the Strategy, its objectives and
  17|             action plans. The Strategy objectives for the next two to three years are:
  18|                        Transforming Teaching and Learning: To ensure teaching and learning are student-centered and inclusive
  19|                        by: (1) establishing effective teams of educational leaders to accomplish the essential work for transforming
  20|                        practice, and (2) hiring and developing excellent educators who are continually learning and adapting practice to
  21|                        meet the needs of all students.
  22|                        Cultivating Climate & Culture: To create a culture that values data use and fosters continuous learning in
  23|                        a climate that is safe, caring, inclusive and reflective of our core values.
  24|                        Communication & Operations: To improve operational efficiencies, including communication, in order to
  25|                        increase the organization's capacity to execute the district's mission and vision.
  26|                        Equity, Access, Achievement & Opportunity for All Students: To ensure all students are provided equal
  27|                        access to a high-quality education, that is collaborative, interactive, and relevant with an appropriate level of
  28|                        support and challenge so that they can become well-rounded, resilient, confident, and capable young adults
  29|                        adequately prepared for future challenges.
  30|             Over the last several years we have worked to put in place the necessary infrastructure to support the academic
  31|             achievement of all students. We have established curriculum leadership positions, instructional specialists/coaches,
  32|             teacher curriculum leaders; piloted and purchased evidence-based math and literacy curricula for Kindergarten (K)
  33|             through grade 5; implemented a standards-based benchmark assessment in math and literacy for grades K through 8;
  34|             provided job-embedded professional development for teachers in best instructional practices and using assessment data
  35|             to inform their instruction; and hired tutoring/intervention staff for additional academic supports during the school day as
  36|             well as after school in order to close learning gaps. The Director of Teaching and Learning and Title I Coordinator
  37|             positions have been and continue to be integral to all of this work.
  38|             In order for students to be ready and able to learn in the classroom, behavioral health needs must be addressed. To that
  39|             end, we have developed a multi-tiered system of added guidance counselors, social workers and adjustment counselors;
  40|             piloted and adopted a districtwide social-emotional learning curriculum. Students receive social-emotional supports in
  41|             small group with counselors or in a one-on-one setting when student need dictates. Additionally, we have continued to
  42|             provide Care Solace, an online resource with live 24/7 concierge meant to assist families and individuals in finding local
  43|             mental health support. Care Solace is a tool for school staff and families to connect with community-based mental
  44|             healthcare resources and providers. The vast database of mental health resources includes vetted local therapists and
  45|             All of these supports have been absolutely critical in order to respond to the increase in significant behavioral health
  46|             needs of our students.
  47|                                                                                              100

===PAGE 101=== (ocr, 50 lines)
   1|                                                                            Superintendent's Message
   2|             The district has been working with intent to foster a safe and caring environment and inclusive culture in our schools and
   3|             district. Last year we administered the National School Climate Center survey for families, students, and staff. We
   4|             analyzed results and used that data to develop goals to improve our schools' climate. We will repeat that survey this
   5|             spring to see if changes have had any impact. As we look to build a more inclusive culture in our schools, we also look
   6|             to diversify our staff to be more reflective of our community. To that end, a district team participated in the Department
   7|             of Elementary and Secondary Education's Teacher Diversification Professional Learning Network and as a result, have
   8|             been examining our recruitment and hiring practices. The Lunenburg School Committee is also committed to this work
   9|             and requested an equity audit for our district. The audit commenced in July 2022 and the final report was made available
  10|             to the Lunenburg community in February 2023 via the district website with a request for feedback. The District
  11|             Leadership will use this report to develop action plans which will be included in the district's Strategy for Continuous
  12|             Improvement. Once these plans are developed, as is our annual practice, the administration will seek the Lunenburg
  13|             School Committee's endorsement. We look forward to engaging in this work with stakeholders.
  14|             Another way the district is seeking to build a more inclusive culture is through strengthening the family-school
  15|             partnership. This year Luneburg Public Schools was one of four districts/schools selected to participate in the
  16|             Department of Elementary and Secondary Education's Family-School Partnership Initiative Program. We will be
  17|             expanding this work to involve stakeholders. This work will also result in the development of action plans, which will be
  18|             incorporated into the Strategy for Continuous Improvement.
  19|             It is important to note that the physical environment of our schools is also important to the learning experience of our
  20|             students. Turkey Hill Elementary School is an aging facility which was not designed for elementary age students. It was
  21|             originally a middle school. The building presents challenges and limitations for our teachers in terms of delivery of
  22|             instruction in the modern era. Electrical outlets are extremely limited in the classrooms and the lack of energy efficient
  23|             windows creates a challenging learning environment with very high and very low outdoor temperatures. The building
  24|             has aging systems that cannot be easily, quickly, or inexpensively addressed. With each passing year, the risk of system
  25|             failures looms larger. It is important for the school district administration, School Committee, and other boards and
  26|             committees to continue to engage in discussion regarding the future of Turkey Hill Elementary School.
  27|             All of these individual efforts and discussions regarding the future are designed to have a cumulative impact on student
  28|             achievement and growth, and overall well-being in the post-COVID years and beyond. I wish to acknowledge and thank
  29|             our staff for their professionalism and flexibility as we have continued to make adjustments in order to provide the best
  30|             possible learning environment and experience for our students; our parents and families for their continued support and
  31|             partnership; and the Lunenburg School Committee, Town Manager, and local boards and committees for their support of
  32|             the schools. We remain focused on improving the ways in which we support our students academically, socially and
  33|             emotionally in partnership with parents, extended families, and the greater community. Through this work we are on a
  34|             journey together. Collectively we have been and will continue to make a difference for our students. I think Maya
  35|             Angelou has captured our journey well when she said, "I have great respect for the past. If you don't know where
  36|             you've come from, you don't know where you're going. I have respect for the past, but I'm a person of the moment.
  37|             I'm here, and I do my best to be completely centered at the place I'm at, then I go forward to the next place."
  38|             Annually I conclude my report with the announcement of the Superintendent's Award recipient. The selection process is
  39|             always difficult as we have many hard-working, high-performing, community service-oriented students. I am pleased to
  40|             announce Cassandra Cohen as the recipient of the 2023 Superintendent's Award. Cassie's academic accomplishments
  41|             include: Academic Excellence Award in Foreign Language, English, History, Mathematics, and Science. She is also a
  42|             Smith Book Award recipient. Cassie has been a member of the Science Olympiad, Golf, and Indoor Track teams. She is
  43|             a member of the World Hunger Task Force and has served as the Class Treasurer. She has also volunteered for the
  44|             Lunenburg Food Pantry, served as a peer tutor, and works as an intern in our district's curriculum office. Since the 8th
  45|             grade, Cassie has been an assistant teacher at the Joseph C. Foster Religious School. When asked for a quote that best
  46|             describes her, she offered this Dolly Parton quote: "Find out who you are and do it on purpose."
  47|             Respectfully Submitted,
  48|             Kate Burnham, Ed.D.
  49|             Superintendent

  15|                                             Lunenburg Public Schools Information Technology Department
  16|             The Lunenburg Public Schools Information Technology (IT) Department maintained a critical role in the expansion of
  17|             the technology in our classrooms. The Department worked with staff and students to redefine what classroom
  18|             instruction looks and feels like for our students.
  19|             The school year began with all students reporting back in session after the COVID restraints with each student having
  20|             access to a device for daily instruction. Here is a summary of the district device model:
  21|                  Lunenburg Primary School - the building have enough iPads to assist with instruction and testing at the K-2 level
  22|                   Turkey Hill Elementary School - each classroom is equipped with Chromebook carts
  23|                Lunenburg Middle High School - each student has access to a personal Chromebook, which they can bring home for
  24|                   their individual studies
  25|             In addition, Turkey Hill Elementary School purchased a Touchview interactive screen for every classroom. This
  26|             equipment assists with the collaboration between teachers and students and has improved student participation.
  27|             Reliance on computers in the classroom and in the households was prevalent when students were burdening the network
  28|             system. With the assistance from E-rate funding, the district purchased new equipment for the building server rooms,
  29|             including routers, switches, wireless access points and licenses.
  30|             Besides expanding educational needs, the IT Department maintained the typical response to maintain the district's core
  31|              echnology services. Our team continues to learn and grow each year as we improve our infrastructure and explore
  32|              nnovations in education technology. The district is still working with a consultant to audit current practices
  33|             applications and models to measure any areas for growth, improvement and capacity.
  34|             Respectfully Submitted,
  35|             Michael Cassidy
  36|             Business Manager/HR Director

   3|             From the March 1, 2022 report (SIMS Report 5-Enrollment Statistics - District Summary), there are 211 students with
   4|             disabilities receiving special education or related services in Lunenburg (last year there were 251). This represents
   5|             13.2% of our total student population of 1,593 students (it was 15.9% last year). The state average from the 2021-22
   6|             Selected Populations Report (https://profiles.doe.mass.edu/statereport/selectedpopulations.aspx) is 19.4%. From the
   7|             March 1, 2022 report (SIMS Report 7-SPED Enrollment Statistics - District Summary), the total number of students
   8|             receiving services outside the district was 8 (last year was 12). Of the 8 students, 4 are placed in Collaborative
   9|             Placements, 2 are in Day Placements and 2 are in Residential Placements. Our Advanced Community Experience
  10|             Program (ACE), which serves students ages 18-22 years old, had 3 students enrolled last year. Additionally, from the
  11|             March 1, 2022 report, 130 students identified with a disability received accommodations via 504 plans in Lunenburg
  12|             (last year was 146).

===PAGE 105=== (ocr, 54 lines)
   1|             Indicator 7 - Early Childhood Outcomes. Districts participate in Indicator 7 data collection as part of a 4 year cycle in
   2|              which they report exit data on children for whom they have collected entry level data; exit may occur at any time during
   3|             the subsequent three year period as the children leave the preschool program. Lunenburg's 2020-2021 cohort reporting
   4|             year results are suppressed for districts reporting 10 or fewer students.
   5|             Indicator 8 - Parent Involvement. This indicator measures the % of parents with a child receiving special education who
   6|             report that school facilitated parent involvement as a means of improving services and results for students with IEPs.
   7|              For 2020-2021 the state target was 89%. Lunenburg's rate was 57.1% and the state's rate was 60.1% (from information
   8|             collected in the Spring of 2019).
   9|             Indicator 9 & 10 - Both of these Indicators deal with Disproportionality in Special Education and in Special Education
  10|              Categories. In Lunenburg, the state determined that there was no disproportionality found in either case.
  11|             Indicator 11 - Initial Evaluation Timelines.                 This indicator measures the % of children with parental consent to evaluate,
  12|             who were evaluated within the State established timeline (2017-2018). Data is reported for all initial evaluations initiated
  13|             in October, November, and December of the reporting year. Lunenburg was rated 90% in compliance and the state
  14|              average for this Indicator was 100%.
  15|             Indicator 12 - Early Childhood Transition. This indicator measures the % of children referred by Part C, found eligible
  16|             for special education services, and who have an IEP developed and implemented by their third birthdays. The state target
  17|             for this indicator in 100% and Lunenburg is noted as "Suppressed" (last school year reviewed was 2017-18). Data is
  18|             suppressed if the number of students referred by Part C and found eligible for special education services is fewer than
  19|             10.
  20|             Indicator 13 - Secondary Transition. In all years, the state target for Secondary Transition is 100%. This indicator
  21|              measures the % of youth with IEPs aged 16 and above with an IEP that includes appropriate measurable postsecondary
  22|             goals that are annually updated and based upon an age appropriate transition assessment, transition services, including
  23|              courses of study, that will reasonably enable the student to meet those postsecondary goals, and annual IEP goals related
  24|             to the student's transition services needs. Lunenburg was last reviewed in this area in 2017-2018 and was 83.3% in
  25|              compliance.
  26|             Indicator 14 - Post-School Outcomes for Students with IEPs. This indicator measures the % of students with IEPs who
  27|              exited high school during the 2019-2020 school year and self-reported post-school engagement in education or
  28|              employment one year after leaving high school. Engagement is defined through three measurements:
  29|             • Measurement A: Enrolled in higher education within one year of leaving high school.
  30|             • Measurement B: Enrolled in higher education or competitively employed within one year of leaving high school.
  31|             • Measurement C: Enrolled in higher education, or in some other postsecondary education or training program; or
  32|                   competitively employed, or in some other employment within one year of leaving high school.
  33|              Measurements are cumulative with Measurement C reflecting the total number of exiters engaged in a post-secondary
  34|              activity one year after leaving high school. For the 2020-2021 school year, state targets for the three measurements of
  35|             Post-School Outcomes are Measurement A: 40.0%; Measurement B: 73.0%; and Measurement C: 79.0%. Lunenburg
  36|             rates are as follows (from reporting year 2020-2021): Measurement A = 50%, Measurement B = 75%, and
  37|             Measurement C = 75%.

  39|             teachers and administration. All parents and community members can join the group, which currently consists of
  40|              parents of students receiving Title I services and parents of students with disabilities. Meetings are currently being held
  41|              quarterly, 6pm, via Zoom. The executive board is made up of a Chairperson: Nichole Camelo.
  42|                        For more information, go to: https://sites.google.com/a/lunenburgschools.net/lps/department-directory/special-
  43|              services/pacsal?authuser=0 In addition to the district PACSAL, Lunenburg also participates in a Regional SEPAC
  44|             group. Information from this group is on Facebook at "Regional SEPAC - Central MA"
  45|             The Lunenburg Public Schools continues to provide a variety of special needs services to meet the varying needs of our
  46|              students in the least restrictive environment. We have an experienced, hard working staff that works together with
  47|              parents to provide these varied services. Our parents are equal partners in our efforts to develop challenging and
  48|              focused educational programs for children. We remain committed to the Lunenburg Public Schools mission,
  49|             mission of the Lunenburg Public Schools is to provide all students with the skills, confidence, and passion for life-long
  50|             learning so that they may find their own paths to successful careers, active citizenship, and rewarding lives." This
  51|             mission, we feel, was even able to be upheld during, and following, the pandemic. Special education services were still
  52|              able to be delivered to students and all DESE recommendations and protocols were put into place and followed in order
  53|             to meet the needs of IEPs as best as possible.

  11|             Respectfully Submitted, Julianna Hanscom
  12|             Director of Special Services
  13|                                                            Teaching & Learning Models and PD Update
  14|             Lunenburg Public Schools returned to a full learning model for the 2021-2022 school year. Students are invested in
  15|             learning, and teachers are working diligently to evaluațe student progress, identify learning gaps, make adjustments to
  16|             practice, and provide scaffolds accordingly. We have implemented structured common planning time for teachers in
  17|             grade level content areas so that teachers may unpack and plan for units of study, align to standards, and look at data and
  18|             student work. Our district literacy specialist and our math instructional specialist meet with teachers during common
  19|             planning time to support curriculum development and help foster student growth. High school department heads
  20|             structure department meeting time to facilitate the necessary curriculum work needed to move each department forward
  21|             and improve learning for students.
  22|             Students in grades K-8 completed the fall and winter iReady diagnostic assessment in both reading and math, and
  23|             teachers are participating in data cycles to analyze data, plan for targeted support, and adjust instruction. Personalized
  24|             learning platforms through iReady provide differentiated instruction, systematic practice, and scaffolded feedback in
  25|             both reading and math.
  26|             The Lunenburg Public School District is participating in the Multi-Tiered Systems of Support (MTSS) Literacy
  27|             Academy and Social Emotional Learning Academy through the Department of Elementary and Secondary Education
  28|             (DESE). The Literacy Academy provides coaching sessions and technical assistance in specific grade levels for literacy,
  29|             including a focus on understanding the science of reading and standards-based lesson planning. Both Academies assist
  30|             our district with creating multi-tiered systems of support with a focus on tier I, II, and III in academics and social
  31|             emotional learning and behavioral health.
  32|             A K-5 math council including grade level teachers, special services teachers, EL teachers, and administrators, has been
  33|             working with DESE's Evaluating and Selecting High-Quality Materials Network to review and select a core math
  34|             curriculum. We are planning to begin a field test and pilot of several programs beginning in February and make a final
  35|             decision regarding purchase this spring.
  36|             Math teachers in grades K-5 have been working with a math consultant and have garnered new strategies for
  37|             mathematical discourse routines. Twenty staff members from throughout the district, including guidance counselors,
  38|             social workers, and teachers were trained and have been implementing the Trails to Wellness curriculum, which is an
  39|             evidence based social emotional wellness program rooted in cognitive behavioral ideology.
  40|             Our Professional Development Committee continues to check-in with the district to evaluate needs and prepare for
  41|             upcoming professional learning including future book studies and workshops. Our administrative team and our
  42|             proceed with this work to include the entire district staff. The team is also participating in a book study based on the
  43|             studying these constructs in connection with our school system and identifying ways we can improve student inclusion
  44|             and experiences in Lunenburg.
  45|             We are pleased to have introduced a new After School Program at the Lunenburg Primary School, Turkey Hill
  46|             Elementary School, Lunenburg Middle School and Lunenburg High School. The program for grades 1-5 provides
  47|             targeted support in literacy and math in addition to a Friday enrichment day. The program for grades 6-12 provides
  48|             academic support for students' current classes in addition to support with executive functioning skills. Our first session
  49|             will be running for seven weeks followed by a second session for an additional ten weeks.
  50|                                                                                              106

===PAGE 108=== (ocr, 45 lines)
   1|                                                                    LUNENBURG PRIMARY SCHOOL
   2|                 The Lunenburg Primary School is a student focused, early childhood, learning community serving students enrolled in
   3|                preschool through grade 2. Student ages range from three to eight years of age. Our community is composed of
   4|                students, families, instructional and support staff. Our focus is on the academic, social emotional growth, and
   5|                development of the whole child.
   6|                 The October 1, 2023 student enrollment reported to the Department of Elementary and Secondary Education was 387
   7|                 students. With 44 preschool students, 106 kindergarten students, 125 first grade students and 112 second grade
   8|                 students. Class sizes range in 5 kindergarten classes from 19-23 students, from 18-22 students in six grade one classes,
   9|                 and from 20-23 in five grade two classes.
  10|                 Our professional development has centered on implementing two new evidenced based curriculums in math and
  11|                literacy from HMH. Into Reading and Into Math. Common Planning Time continues to be a priority for our building
  12|                schedule and the re-allocation of existing support staff to address student needs based on student performance data.
  13|                 We have been able to access consultants as well district curriculum coordinators so that teachers have an
  14|                opportunity as a whole grade level to develop and provide evidence based instruction, common learning
  15|                experiences, student data analysis and share effective teaching strategies to support student academic
  16|                growth.
  17|                Our staff works to maintain a school culture, which is positive, inclusive, and supportive. We continue to implement
  18|                 the Responsive Classroom model in all of our classrooms. Positive Behavioral Interventions and Supports (PBIS) is
  19|                an evidence-based three-tiered framework for improving and integrating all of the data, systems, and practices
  20|                affecting student outcomes every day. We continue to use Trails to Wellness, which is a whole-school/district-wide
  21|                approach to teaching social emotional learning. It is designed to help students recognize, understand,
  22|                describe, communicate and regulate their emotions and provide them with the tools they need to create and
  23|                maintain positive social interaction with their peers.
  24|                 The Primary School is a Title One school and we have continued our tiered instructional system to ensure that all
  25|                students have the services they need to be successful. Common assessments are completed in the fall, winter, and
  26|                spring for all children in grades K-2 in the areas of reading, writing, and math. These common assessments serve to monitor the effectiveness of teacher instruction and the progress of students. Struggling students are identified based
  27|                 on assessment data for access to our current Multi-Tiered Support System (MTSS) in Reading, Math, Speech,
  28|                Occupational and Physical Therapy. The MTSS model is also used to provide social emotional support for students
  29|                that are referred to our building Behavioral Health Support Team.
  30|                The Primary School Council meets each month to discuss school related concerns. Serving as an advisory group to
  31|                the principal, the council is charged with reviewing the school building budget and developing the school
  32|                improvement plan.
  33|                Members of the Council for the 2022-2023 school year are:
  34|                Principal:                                                                    Chad Adams
  35|                 Assistant Principal:                                                         Gina Kutarnia
  36|                 Teachers:                                                      Kirsten Snape, Kara Slattery, Serene Pease, Melanie Roy, Tara Bastien
  37|                 Parents:                                                           Meaghan Arnold, Shaunna Baril, Ashley Steele
  38|                School Committee Rep:                                               Laura Kelly
  39|                 We are truly grateful for the opportunity to work with our highly dedicated district wide colleagues and a caring
  40|                community in order to provide the best possible educational opportunities for our youngest students.
  41|                For more information about the work that we do at the Lunenburg Primary School please visit our website: https://
  42|                sites.google.com/a/lunenburgschools.net/primary/
  43|                Respectfully submitted,
  44|                Chad Adams, Principal

===PAGE 110=== (ocr, 51 lines)
   1|                                                                        Turkey Hill Elementary School
   2|             It has been a remarkable year of recovery and growth for our school community. A full two years after the COVID
   3|              epidemic so profoundly affected all of our personal and professional lives; we finally had the luxury to implement
   4|             "normal" protocols again in our classrooms. The masks became voluntary, social distancing officially ended, Plexiglas
   5|              shields were stored away, and children were allowed to work and play with their peers without restrictions. In March,
   6|              we unrolled our classroom carpets, grouped desks together for collaborative learning, and many other restrictions were
   7|              eliminated. We invited parents back into our building with both a Memorial Day celebration in May and moving up
   8|              ceremony for fifth graders in June. All students enjoyed exciting new experiences on field trips: 3°                                       graders to the
   9|              Ecotarium, 4* graders to Leominster State Forest, and Sth                      graders to Polar Park in Worcester for a WooSox game. It was
  10|             wonderful to operate as a fully functioning, fully present elementary school again.
  11|              In the fall of 2022 Turkey Hill Elementary School had 358 enrolled students. There are five sections of grade 3 and
  12|              grade 5, with a total of 111 students and 105 students respectively. There are six sections of grades 4 with 142 students.
  13|             We employ 27 teachers, 100% of whom are licensed in their assignment area. Our special areas include physical
  14|             education, art, music, library, STEM, and social emotional learning. We are also served by a full time assistant principal,
  15|              two full time nurses, a psychologist, BCBA, a full time adjustment counselor/social worker and a guidance counselor.
  16|             Other support personnel who work across the district include a literacy specialist/Title 1 coordinator, a math specialist,
  17|             the Director of Teaching and Learning, two EL teachers, a speech and language pathologist, OT, and PT. We continue to
  18|              benefit from the services of an interventionist and two supporting tutors. They work under the direction of our literacy
  19|             specialist and provide targeted support for small groups of students. Our talented front office administrative assistant,
  20|             Ms. McAllister, effectively keeps all of us organized, ensuring that students' after school and dismissal plans are
  21|             conveyed to teachers, welcoming new families and helping them through registration, and keeping administrators on
  22|             track each day.
  23|             Staff rededicated themselves to our mission of providing all students with the skills, confidence and passion for lifelong
  24|             learning, and students focused to develop their skills in literacy and math, social studies and science, as well as the
  25|              special area subjects. Beyond that, our school enjoyed several enriching events.
  26|             In January, May and September, all students participated in the iReady reading and math benchmark assessments, and
  27|             we celebrated students' academic growth and effort. We continue to address the negative impact of remote learning,
  28|              and data provided by these assessments helps refine our focus for interventions.
  29|             In March and April, THES and Primary School staff collaborated under the guidance of curriculum director Annica
  30|              Scott and literacy coach/Title 1 coordinator Deidre Mclean to review, trial and finally select research based, standards
  31|              aligned curriculum for both math and science.
  32|             They chose Houghton Mifflin Harcourt's Into Reading and Into Math, and over the summer many teachers started to
  33|              familiarize themselves with the expansive elements of the programs.
  34|             Our annual spelling bee was held in February, and Miss Chloe McKenna withstood the challenges of unfamiliar words
  35|             and several fierce competitors to prevail as our school spelling champion.
  36|             In March, 4 grade parents and students were introduced to a new iteration of Nature's Classroom by Mrs. Rhonda
  37|              Connery, in preparation for a fall week at Grotonwood. Every 5" student except a few away on vacation participated in
  38|             the Nature's Classroom during the last week of September, with the additional option of attending only the day program
  39|              or staying overnight. There were some tears shed on the Monday bus ride to Groton waving good-bye to parents and a
  40|              few more tears fell on the Friday bus ride home, departing from their camp counselors, as new bonds of friendship were
  41|              formed. It was a successful week with good weather, community building, and the creation of fond memories of a
  42|             unique time together, thanks to the combined efforts of our entire fifth grade staff.
  43|              We held a fully in-person open house in early September (the first in three years). It was a well-attended event and for
  44|              many parents their first opportunity to be physically present in our building. It was accompanied by a book fair
  45|              facilitated by our new librarian, Mrs. Mack. Parent conferences in October offered both in person and virtual options and
  46|              appointments were fully booked within a few days.
  47|             Other highlights from the fall season included the Girls on The Run program, a research based program designed to help
  48|              girls understand themselves better, value their relationships and teamwork, and recognize how they can shape the world
  49|             to be a better place! Over 30 girls participated, with the assistance of many parent and high school volunteers, headed by
  50|             Coach Nichole Camelo.
  51|                                                                                              110

===PAGE 111=== (ocr, 31 lines)
   1|              We held our annual Penny Wars fundraiser to help purchase exciting new technology for our classrooms. As always, our
   2|             PTO parents and volunteers assisted in the weeklong collection, headed by parent Alex Lind; we could not be successtul
   3|             without their efforts.
   4|             All sixteen homerooms as well as our five special education rooms were outfitted with Touch View Panels. These 65
   5|             inch interactive displays allow teachers to create and demonstrate any content to the class from their computers
   6|             Text is easier to read. Students can manipulate the on-screen content as a team and problem solve. These have become
   7|             essential tools for the classrooms displaying the new literacy and math curricula digital content.
   8|             We were able to bring back a fifth grade band and chorus program under the direction of our new music teacher, Mrs.
   9|             Alice Daugherty. The chorus delighted our veteran guests at the Veteran's Day ceremony with their performance of an
  10|             Armed Forces medley of the United States. There were 39 veteran guests, relatives of our students, who honored us with
  11|             their presence on November 10th
  12|             Our assistant principal, Mr. John Palumbo, has led the staff in the effective implementation of both PBIS and restorative
  13|             justice at Turkey Hill. Positive Behavioral Intervention Supports (PBIS) involves using evidence-based prevention and
  14|             intervention practices to support academic, social, emotional and behavioral competence of all students. THES has
  15|             teamed with the Primary School to provide similar protocols and language about behavior expectations across all school
  16|             areas, which are modeled and agreed upon with visual reminders throughout the school.
  17|             Restorative justice focuses on mediation and agreement rather than punishment. It's about accepting responsibility for
  18|             harm and making restitution. Our focus has shifted from a penalty to "how can we make it right?" Students have been
  19|             remarkably engaged in offering apologies, cleaning up, and making appropriate amends. Staff also use incentive
  20|             programs for struggling students to help manage challenging behaviors and develop their self-confidence and self-
  21|             From September to December, 2021 there were 83 discipline referrals and 7 suspensions. From September to December,
  22|             2022 there were 53 discipline referrals and 4 suspensions. We attribute this impressive 36% decrease in referrals to the
  23|             efforts of the staff to implement PBIS over the last two years. We have also noticed fewer repeat offenders as restorative
  24|             justice is teaching students about accepting responsibility for their behavior.
  25|             Our school's success relies upon the combined and collaborative efforts of our wonderful staff, parents, and community
  26|             members. We continue to seek out opportunities to engage and effectively communicate with our families. Turkey Hill
  27|             may be a well-worn building, constructed in 1969, but it is also a well-loved and cared for community of students
  28|             supported by parents, staff, and Lunenburg residents. I am honored to serve as principal.
  29|             Respectfully Submitted,
  30|             Heidi Champagne, Principal

===PAGE 113=== (ocr, 46 lines)
   1|                                                                            Lunenburg Middle School
   2|             Lunenburg Middle School provides a rigorous academic curriculum as well as strong programs that support students'
   3|             social and emotional growth. These programs ensure that each child becomes a self-motivated, creative, lifelong learner
   4|             in a safe, stimulating environment that encourages diversity and open honest communication. The dedicated and
   5|             accomplished staff of professionals within the school embraces the philosophy of educating the whole child
   6|             academically, socially, and emotionally. This philosophy is a means by which all students become positive community
   7|             members that possess the skills to be successful in high school and beyond.
   8|              Lunenburg Middle School is in its sixth year as a co-located middle - high school serving grades six through eight.
   9|             Effective, student centered middle schools embrace interdisciplinary teams of teachers that enable teachers to work with
  10|             smaller groups of students. Smaller groups of students allow teachers to learn and engage students in their preferred
  11|             learning style, making them more confident and successful.
  12|             Our effective middle school model is student centered and provides opportunities to explore a variety of different
  13|             interests and talents through special areas and enrichment classes and extracurricular activities. One of our goals is for
  14|             students to discover something they are passionate about and want to continue exploring as they move through high
  15|             school and beyond. We will continue to seek out opportunities for students that foster critical thinking and problem
  16|             solving skills that prepare them for high school and beyond or where their passion may lead them.
  17|                                                                                        August 2022
  18|             With the return to a sense of normalcy at the beginning of the school year, we are fortunate to not have any mandated
  19|             health or safety protocols associated with the global pandemic. We have identified that a large number of students
  20|             suffered some type of "learning loss" during the course of the pandemic. This means students may not be functioning at
  21|             grade level in the areas of ELA and Math.
  22|             In order to combat this learning loss, LMS is utilizing the iReady learning platform to provide an additional learning
  23|             component in addition to classroom instruction. iReady is being implemented during our intervention blocks and
  24|             provides teachers with individualized learning paths based upon the students individual learning needs. Parents will be
  25|             provided fall, winter and spring benchmark assessments created by iReady so they too can monitor student progress.
  26|             Lunenburg Middle School has benefited from a top-notch guidance support team that we have had in place since 2021.
  27|             Two guidance counselors, a school social worker coupled with our shared school psychologist has been able to provide a
  28|             level of social emotional support to our students and their families that we have not been accustomed to. Data supports
  29|             the need for these positions as during the pandemic, the number of student's experiencing anxiety and depression has
  30|             significantly increased nationwide and in our own community. Our guidance team has been able to meet the needs of our
  31|             students while preparing them to be available for academic learning.
  32|             Our grade eight team of teachers continue to implement a schedule that mirrors the high school schedule. Our
  33|             philosophy is to ease the difficult transition from middle school to the first year of high school by developing student's
  34|             independent skills they will require to be successful in high school. Grade eight teachers function as one inclusive team
  35|             where students take classes with a variety of different teachers as they would in a high school setting. This differs from
  36|             our two interdisciplinary team of teachers that have been a successful model in grades six and seven.
  37|                                                                                 Spring MCAS Score

===PAGE 114=== (ocr, 21 lines)
   1|              2022 Spring MCAS testing at LMS show the percentage of students who scored in the " meeting or Exceeding"
   2|              category and then the column next to it shows our scores in comparison with the state scores in schools who service
   3|             similar grade levels. As you can see, even with the effects of the pandemic, LMS scores are above the state percentage
   4|             in six out of the seven areas tested. In grade 8 Science Technology and Engineering our scores are over 20 points
   5|             higher than the state average, an impressive number. We look forward to what 2023 scores will indicate after the
   6|             specific interventions we have put in place for students to be successful.
   7|                                                                                 Athletics and clubs
   8|             LMS successfully participated in middle school Field Hockey and Boys and Girls Cross Country and this winter we
   9|             will continue with middle school basketball after many years of it being eliminated. The opportunities middle school
  10|               ports provides to students to participate, learn and become part of an effective team atmosphere is immeasurable
  11|               cience Olvmpiad. Drama Club. Yearbook Club and middle school Student Government return as tavorite activitie

===PAGE 115=== (ocr, 42 lines)
   1|                                                                              Lunenburg High School
   2|             Lunenburg High School and its faculty continue to focus on rigorous curriculum, high standards and opportunities for
   3|             students, both academic and extracurricular, so that every student feels supported and included in our community. LHS
   4|             faculty strives to reflect on their teaching practices, to learn and to change in order to best serve all of our students.
   5|                                                                                 2022-2023 School year
   6|             In August of 2022, Lunenburg High School was able to return to school without health and safety protocols due to the
   7|             pandemic for the first time in two and a half years. Our entire school community did an outstanding job of educating
   8|             students through the pandemic, our efforts now focus on identifying students who were struggling and providing
   9|             educational services that would help them succeed in their high school careers and beyond.
  10|             One of the components of that support was the addition of a LHS Intervention Teacher. This teaching position was
  11|             created in order to provide direct instruction to students who were identified by their teachers as to needing additional
  12|             support to be successful in the classroom. Students report to the Interventionist during their study skills block and
  13|             receive assistance to the specific class or classes they are struggling in. The initial data collected as to the effectiveness
  14|             of this position and the direct impact on students classroom grades and learning are remarkable.
  15|             LHS also adopted an additional Algebra Essentials II class that was developed to ensure specific students were able to
  16|             access the curriculum and state standards. Creating this additional class benefited all students, as it was able to reduce
  17|             class sizes in Algebra II and allow teachers to provide more personalized, individualized instruction while working with
  18|             smaller groups of students in a traditionally academically demanding course.
  19|             The addition to the faculty and courses at Lunenburg High School have allowed students a more varied options for
  20|             course selection, provided individualized and programmatic support and structure for students to be successful. LHS
  21|             looks forward to reporting out on the difference in student learning these positions have made.
  22|                                                                                   MCAS RESULTS

===PAGE 121=== (ocr, 40 lines)
   1|                                     Letter from Leadership
   2|                                                     The 2021-2022 school year was my eighth year serving as School Principal at Monty Tech,
   3|                                                     and I am honored that it concluded with my appointment to a new role - that of
   4|                                                     Superintendent-Director. Having been part of this remarkable educational community,
   5|                                                    developing a deep understanding of initiatives - both large and small - that are an integral
   6|                                                     part of our educational programs, and working with and learning from our outstanding
   7|                                                    faculty, staff and administrative team, has allowed for a successful transition into this new
   8|                                                     position. I could not be more grateful for the encouraging and kind remarks I continue to
   9|                                                    receive from students and parents, faculty and staff, and the regional business community,
  10|                                                     whose support means so much to the Monty Tech family.
  11|                                                    With the effects of the pandemic still being felt, students and staff were encouraged to
  12|                                                    maintain health and safety standards learned in the previous year, but to also return to a new
  13|             "normal." Daily in-person instruction, off-campus construction projects, and Skills competitions returned. While the
  14|             pandemic certainly brought questions, fears, and insecurities to the surface for many, it also provided instructional staff
  15|             with opportunities to collaborate and develop new skills in infusing technology into daily lessons.                                                Monty Tech
  16|             instructors worked tirelessly, with a new appreciation for instructional technology, to embed some of "what worked" into
  17|             their daily, class instruction. Collecting assignments via Google Classrooms, conducting quick learning assessments via
  18|             apps like Quizizz or Kahoot, and assigning group learning projects using Google Slides and Docs, teachers resulted in a
  19|             heightened level of student engagement.
  20|             The regional workforce also seemed to find its footing in FY22, welcoming approximately 200 students into local shops
  21|             and businesses to work and learn alongside industry professionals, through the school's long-standing co-op program.
  22|             Another remarkable example of school-to-business collaboration was demonstrated when eight businesses submitted
  23|             letters of support to Commonwealth Corporation on the school's behalf. Attesting to their belief in Monty Tech training
  24|             programs, agreeing to interview program completers, and even highlighting Monty Tech graduates currently on stati,
  25|             these letters were instrumental in the acquisition of a $600,000 grant award, which would provide no-cost training for
  26|             young adults throughout our area. Monty Tech understands the value of a vocation, and through the Career Technical
  27|             Initiative, we have been able to extend training opportunities beyond the school day, helping a wide range of adults train
  28|             for those high wage, high skills jobs that remained unfilled when the pandemic subsided.
  29|             As the value of a vocational education becomes more evident, we have seen applications to attend Monty Tech on the
  30|             rise. While we understand that frustration that can come with not being admitted to a school of your choosing, we are
  31|             challenging ourselves to work with local school leaders to establish practices and programs that will ensure as many
  32|             students as possible are admitted from each sending community, and that all students who are interested in the school
  33|             have an opportunity to visit and tour our campus. Undoubtedly, a highlight of the 2021-2022 school year was seeing all
  34|             eighth grade students from Fitchburg Public Schools join our tour day event. I look forward to further discussions and
  35|             collaborations with area school leaders, so that we may establish innovative training programs that will serve those who
  36|             I remain humbled and honored to lead such an outstanding organization, and thank you for your continued support.
  37|             Respectfully submitted,
  38|                 Thomas 11. Browe
  39|             Thomas R. Browne, Superintendent-Director
  40|                                                                                              121

   1|                                                 Financial Report

   7|             In an effort to develop a cost-effective budget for the fiscal year 2021-2022, a great deal of effort was put forth by the
   8|             School Committee, administration, and staff. The final fiscal year 2021-2022 Educational Plan totaled $28,605,425
   9|             which represents a 0.73% decrease over the 2020-2021 Educational Plan. The District's FY22 budget only exceeds the
  10|             minimum spending required by Massachusetts General Law Chapter 70 by $203,320 or 0.78%.
  11|              To supplement the local budget, school officials pursue grant funding to provide valuable educational and social
  12|             services to the diverse student population. For fiscal year 2022, state and federal grant sources provided the school with
  13|             $2,927,860. Programs funded by these grants include: Elementary and Secondary Schools Emergency Relief Funds,
  14|             Improving Teacher Quality, Special Education Services and Program Improvement, Title I Support, Perkins
  15|             Occupational Education, Vocational Capital Equipment Initiatives, Life Sciences, Student Support and Academic
  16|             Enrichment and Marine Corp Junior Reserve Officer Training.
  17|             The District is participating in an annual financial audit, conducted by the accounting firm of Melanson, Heath and Co.
  18|             from Greenfield, Massachusetts. School officials anticipate a good report.

   1|                                              Attending Manty Tech
   2|             As vocational schools across the Commonwealth grow in popularity, and the value of vocational training becomes more
   3|             evident, we have seen a steady increase in the
   4|             number of students applying for admission to                                                    Students Applying for Admission
   5|             Monty Tech.                                                       1000
   6|                                                                                900                                                                                  861
   7|             With seats available limited only by our                           800                                             775               754
   8|             building capacity, and a deep understanding                        700        614
   9|             of the increasing demand, school leaders                                                        607
  10|                                                                                600
  11|             continue to work with area school and                              500
  12|             business leaders to develop and expand                             400             375                359               355               371                 370
  13|             programs to address this concern. Whether                          300
  14|             these new programs are offered in the                              200
  15|             evening through the School of Continuing                           100
  16|             Education, or even during the traditional
  17|             school day, our goal is to expand access so                                     2018              2019               2020               2021              2022
  18|             that students who hope to enter the workforce
  19|             may do so with a set of valuable skills and
  20|             industry-recognized
  21|             make them more employable, and better equipped to grow and learn in the occupation of their choosing.
  22|             While Monty Tech continues to ensure that students are job ready immediately after graduation, not all students who
  23|             graduate from Monty Tech choose to enter the workforce upon completion. In fact, several of the school's vocational
  24|             training programs prepare students for advanced education and training in high-wage, high-skill careers in engineering,
  25|             allied health, business, education and computer sciences that demand a bachelor's and even master's degree. As such, it
  26|             is imperative that the school also prepare those students for college level coursework.
  27|             Students who attend Monty Tech must successfully complete all of the same academic requirements that students in
  28|             comprehensive high schools complete, but at Monty Tech, students are only in academic classes every other week.
  29|             Many students may find adjusting to this week on - week off schedule challenging, but it is this same schedule that
  30|             provides Monty Tech students with time to explore and learn in a chosen vocational program at a deeper level. Whether
  31|             running the school's on-site, full-service restaurant, operating the School Store, building a home for Habitat for
  32|             Humanity, or performing community services across our sending communities, Monty Tech students are developing
  33|             technical skills, while also learning critical time management and study skills.                                     We are confident that whether our
  34|             graduates choose to pursue college or career pathways, they are prepared for what lies ahead.

===PAGE 125=== (ocr, 36 lines)
   1|                                              Academics
   2|             During the spring of 2022, 10th grade students took the Next Generation MCAS examination in English Language Arts,
   3|             Mathematics, and Biology. In addition, select 9th graders also sat for the Next Generation MCAS Biology exam.
   4|             Students performed well across all subject areas, with a passing rate of 100% in English Language Arts, 97% in
   5|             Mathematics, and 97% in Biology.
   6|             While Monty Tech is certainly known for its strong vocational training programs, its academic programs continue to
   7|             evolve to prepare graduates for diverse and challenging college and career pathways, and are widely known to
   8|             effectively prepare students at all levels to reach their goals. A focus in recent years has been the incorporation of new
   9|             and updated materials and coursework to expand STEM opportunities for students. In FY22, Monty Tech applied for a
  10|             Massachusetts Life Science Center grant, developed in collaboration with our post-secondary partners at Fitchburg State
  11|             University. Using the $110,000 award, Monty Tech instructors will work collaboratively to align course expectations
  12|             with FU's Anatomy & Physiology class, laying a foundation for a first-ever articulation agreement with FSU.
  13|             Instructors look forward to participating in curriculum development activities at the University, and will attend related,
  14|             immersive professional development programs to build capacity and collect/share valuable expertise from colleagues.
  15|             Additionally, when the proposed agreement (Dual Enrollment or Articulation) is finalized, students who successfully
  16|             complete the course will earn 4 college credits, saving them time and as much as $1,900 as they consider pursuing
  17|             STEM college pathways.
  18|              Monty Tech students also benefitted from the establishment of another new science course in FY22, Microbiology.
  19|             Instructors develope the new curriculum ana travelled to Texas to participate in the National Science Teaching
  20|             Association Annual Conference, while school officials renovated a science lab to replicate a biomedical facility. All new
  21|             course materials, equipment and technology was purchased and the addition of a valuable STEM opportunity has been a
  22|             welcome addition to an already rigorous science program.
  23|             In May of 2022, 120 students participated in AP Exams. Those 120 students took 162 AP Exams collectively. An
  24|             impressive 82 of the 120 students were eligible to receive college credit for qualifying scores of 3+. That is a 68.33%
  25|             pass rate, which is higher than the global average and a significant increase from the previous school year.
  26|             Additionally, to support the expansion of AP course offerings, three instructors completed the training to teach AP Seminar during the summer of 2022. AP Seminar is now offered as a co-taught elective course for juniors. As an
  27|             extension of AP Seminar, instructors will be trained to teach AP Research during the summer of 2023. Together, AP
  28|             Seminar and AP Capstone will allow MT students to qualify for the AP Capstone designation upon graduation. Students
  29|             who earn the AP Capstone designation are highly qualified for college success, and thus, more likely to gain admission
  30|             into competitive colleges and universities.
  31|                                                                                  2018                 2019               2020                  2021                2022
  32|                                               Total AP Students                    95                  113                   106                 142                   120
  33|                                               Number of Exams                      134                  148                  146                  189                  162
  34|                                AP Students with Scores 3+                           46                   69                   75                   61                   82
  35|               % of Total AP Students with Scores 3+                             48.42%               61.06%               70.75%               42.96%              68.33%
  36|                                                                                              125

===PAGE 126=== (ocr, 53 lines)
   1|                Vocational Training
   2|             While students and staff at Monty Tech have always known the value of a vocational education, it may have taken a
   3|             pandemic and the return to in-person learning in 2022 to be reminded of the significance and benefit of hands-on
   4|             learning.
   5|             Students and staff across the twenty-one different vocational-technical education programs gladly returned to the
   6|             important work of building homes, reconstructing damaged properties, repairing service vehicles, making walkways

  28|                                               Cooperative @ducation
  29|             The Monty Tech Cooperative Education Program is an extension of the student's technical education that combines
  30|             classroom instruction with on-the-job-training. The popular Co-op Program provides students with an opportunity to
  31|             further develop academic, technical and employability skills in an industry-related work environment. All students are
  32|             eligible to participate in the Co-op Program, provided they satisty state and school grade, attendance and pertormance
  33|             requirements, and all Co-op students have completed the OSHA recognized Career Safe online health and safety course
  34|             prior to being placed in a work environment.
  35|             The Cooperative Education Program is beneficial for both student and employer. For the student, it is a chance to gain
  36|             on-the-job training that improves both their technical proficiency                             and employability skills,
  37|             connections enhances post-graduate employment opportunities.                             The Co-op Program also allows students to earn while
  38|             they learn, which has allowed many students to learn valuable life skills, financial literacy skills, and gain a deeper
  39|             understanding of the importance of career advancement and self-advocacy.
  40|             Employer benefits include addressing workforce needs in an efficient manner. Co-op students provide a pool of trained talent,
  41|             ready to assume temporary, part-time or even full-time opportunities, thus reducing employer training costs. Co-op work hours may
  42|             also be tailored to suit the needs of partnering employers.
  43|             In 2021-2022, the school saw a return to the workplace, and signiticant increases in the number of students placed in this
  44|             valuable training program. With approximately 100 area employers participatıng in Cooperative Education, providing
  45|             valuable experience to almost 200 students by the end of the 2021-2022 school year, school officials remain grateful for
  46|             their continued support, and look forward to future collaborations.
  47|                                                                                              130
